Court
Type

Every motion and deadline-bearing procedure a party meets in Missouri civil, criminal and appellate practice and in the federal courts — what it is, what kind of motion it is, when it is used, the deadline, and the rule that sets it. Each deadline was read from the current rule or statute text on 9 September 2026 and links to the source.

254 entries244 verified against rule text10 where no rule sets a deadline or the text could not be retrieved16 that a court cannot extend

How to read it. The left column is the clock: the deadline in plain terms and the rule. The right column is the motion: what it does, when it is the right tool, and what happens if the deadline passes. Type tells you what kind of motion it is — dispositive motions end all or part of a case; procedural ones shape it; discovery motions get evidence; post-judgment and appellate motions are the ones with the hardest clocks. Filters narrow by court and type; the search box matches anything in an entry, including rule numbers.

Three cautions. A judge's scheduling order or standing order, and the circuit's local rules, can set an earlier date than the rule — they win. Federal rules amend every December 1 and Missouri rules amended several times in 2024–2025, so anything time-critical should be re-read at the source link before filing. And this is a reference, not legal advice: it tells you what the rule says, not what to do in your case.

The three clocks that catch people

Missouri civil 53

Pleading 19

30 days after service of the summons and petition.
If service was by mail: 30 days after the acknowledgment of receipt or the return registered/certified mail receipt is filed in the case. If neither personal service nor service by mail was had: 45 days after the first publication of notice. Answer to a cross-claim: 30 days after it is filed. Reply: 30 days after filing of the pleading it is directed to; if a reply is ordered by the court, 20 days after entry of the order unless the order directs otherwise.
Rule 55.25(a)-(b)

Answer / responsive pleading (not a motion, but the clock everything else hangs on)

ProceduralVerified

The defendant's required responsive pleading admitting or denying the petition's allegations and stating affirmative defenses.

When to use it After being served with a summons and petition, unless you instead file a Rule 55.27 motion.

If you miss it You are in default and the plaintiff may take an interlocutory order of default and then a default judgment under Rule 74.05.

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 55.25(c): filing a Rule 55.27 motion resets the clock — if the motion is denied or taken with the case, the responsive pleading is due 10 days after notice of the court's action; if a motion for more definite statement is granted, 10 days after the more definite statement is filed.

Pro se note Calendar the 30 days the day you are served. Rule 44.01(d) adds 3 days only when the paper was served BY MAIL — being served with the original summons in person adds nothing.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199656

Rule 55.27(a): the motion must be made '(A) …
Within the time allowed for responding to the opposing party's pleading, or (B) If no responsive pleading is permitted, within thirty days after the service of the last pleading.' In practice that is the same 30 days you would have to answer under Rule 55.25(a).
Rule 55.27(a)(1)-(11); Rule 55.27(c) (preliminary hearings); Rule 55.25(a)

Motion to dismiss (Rule 55.27(a) defenses)

DispositiveVerified

A motion raising one or more of the eleven threshold defenses listed in Rule 55.27(a) instead of answering — most commonly failure to state a claim, lack of jurisdiction, and defective process or service.

When to use it When the petition is legally deficient on its face, the court lacks jurisdiction, or process/service was bad, and you want that decided before you answer.

If you miss it Most of the eleven defenses are WAIVED — see Rule 55.27(g). Waiting also puts you at risk of default if you file nothing at all.

Notes & source

Response / reply No response/reply times are set by Rule 55.27 itself. Rule 44.01(c) requires a written motion and notice of hearing to be served not later than 5 days before the hearing unless a different period is fixed.

Pro se note The 11 grounds are: (1) lack of subject matter jurisdiction; (2) lack of personal jurisdiction; (3) plaintiff lacks legal capacity to sue; (4) insufficiency of process; (5) insufficiency of service of process; (6) failure to state a claim; (7) failure to join a party under Rule 52.04; (8) plaintiff should furnish security for costs; (9) another action pending between the same parties for the same cause in this state; (10) several claims improperly united; (11) counterclaim or cross-claim cannot properly be interposed. Rule 55.27(c) lets you ask for these to be heard and determined BEFORE trial.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199658

No separate deadline. The waiver bites at the moment you file your first Rule 55.27 motion (55.27(f)) …
or at the moment your responsive pleading is filed without the defense (55.27(g)(1)).
Rule 55.27(f); Rule 55.27(g)(1)-(3)

Waiver trap — Rule 55.27(f) and (g)

ProceduralVerified

The consolidation and waiver rules: if you file a Rule 55.27 motion and leave out an available Rule 55.27 defense, you cannot raise it by later motion, and defenses neither moved on nor pleaded in a responsive pleading are waived.

When to use it Read this before you file any Rule 55.27 motion — it determines what you must put in the FIRST motion.

If you miss it The omitted defense is gone. Three defenses survive longer under 55.27(g)(2) — failure to state a claim, failure to join an indispensable party, and failure to state a legal defense — and may be raised in any pleading permitted under Rule 55.01 or by motion for judgment on the pleadings. Under 55.27(g)(3) the court 'shall dismiss the action' whenever it appears it lacks subject matter jurisdiction, so that one is never waived.

Notes & source

Pro se note Put EVERY Rule 55.27 ground you have into one motion. Splitting them across two motions forfeits the second one.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199658

Made before filing your responsive pleading, within the time allowed for responding to the pleading (Rule 55.27(a)(A)).
If the court orders a more definite statement and the order is not obeyed within 10 days after notice of the order, the court may strike the pleading or make such order as it deems just.
Rule 55.27(d)

Motion for more definite statement

ProceduralVerified

A motion asking the court to order the other side to replead because their pleading is so vague you cannot frame a responsive pleading or prepare for trial.

When to use it When a petition is so indefinite you genuinely cannot answer it — not merely because you want more detail (use discovery for that).

If you miss it You lose the right to raise it by motion under Rule 55.27(f) and must answer the pleading as written.

Notes & source

Response / reply If granted, your responsive pleading is due 10 days after the more definite statement is filed (Rule 55.25(c)).

Pro se note Judges rarely grant these. If you can answer at all, answer and use interrogatories instead.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199658

Rule 55.27(e): 'Upon motion made by a party before responding to a pleading or …
if no responsive pleading is permitted by these rules, upon motion made by a party within thirty days after the service of the pleading.' The court may also act on its own initiative at any time.
Rule 55.27(e)

Motion to strike

ProceduralVerified

A motion to remove insufficient defenses or redundant, immaterial, impertinent or scandalous matter from a pleading.

When to use it To clean up an opponent's pleading, or to strike a legally insufficient affirmative defense.

If you miss it The objectionable matter stays in the pleading; the motion is untimely if made after you respond.

Notes & source

Pro se note File it BEFORE you answer. Once your answer is on file the window for a party motion has closed.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199658

Rule 55.27(b): 'After the pleadings are closed but within such time as not to delay the trial, any party may move for judgment on the pleadings.' No fixed day count.
Rule 55.27(b); see also Rule 55.27(g)(2)

Motion for judgment on the pleadings

DispositiveVerified

A motion asking the court to enter judgment based only on the pleadings, because even taking the opponent's well-pleaded facts as true they lose as a matter of law.

When to use it After the answer (and any reply) is filed, when the pleadings themselves establish who wins.

If you miss it No waiver of the underlying defense, but you lose the chance to end the case cheaply and will have to try it or move for summary judgment.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule. Rule 44.01(c) 5-day notice-of-hearing rule applies.

Pro se note Unlike summary judgment, you cannot attach evidence. If you attach exhibits outside the pleadings the court may treat it as a Rule 74.04 summary judgment motion.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199658

Rule 55.33(a): a party may amend once as a matter of course at any time before a responsive pleading is served;
if the pleading is one to which no responsive pleading is permitted and the action has not been placed upon the trial calendar, the party may so amend it within 30 days after it is served. Otherwise, only by leave of court or written consent of the adverse party; leave 'shall be freely given when justice so requires.'
Rule 55.33(a); relation back under Rule 55.33(c); amendment to conform to the evidence under Rule 55.33(b); supplemental pleadings under Rule 55.33(d)

Amendment as of right / motion for leave to amend a pleading

ProceduralVerified

Changing your own petition, answer or counterclaim — once for free early on, and thereafter only with the opponent's written consent or the court's leave.

When to use it When you discover a new claim, a new defense, a misnamed party, or a pleading defect the other side has attacked.

If you miss it You are stuck with the pleading you have; late amendments can be refused as prejudicial, and a claim added after limitations runs may be time-barred unless it relates back under Rule 55.33(c).

Notes & source

Response / reply A party shall plead in response to an amended pleading within the time remaining for response to the original pleading or within 10 days after service of the amended pleading, whichever period may be the longer, unless the court orders otherwise.

Pro se note Rule 55.33(b) lets the court permit amendment to conform to the evidence 'even after judgment,' and Rule 55.33(c) can relate an amendment back to the original filing date — critical if the statute of limitations has run.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199664

'Upon timely application' — Rule 52.12(a) and (b) set a timeliness standard, not a day count. No fixed number of days is stated in the rule.
Rule 52.12(a) (intervention of right); Rule 52.12(b) (permissive); Rule 52.12(c) (procedure)

Motion to intervene

ProceduralVerified

A non-party's motion to be allowed into a pending case, either as of right (a statute confers it, or your interest may be impaired and nobody is adequately representing you) or permissively (common question of law or fact).

When to use it When a lawsuit you are not part of will decide something you own or are entitled to.

If you miss it An untimely application is denied and the case is decided without you; you may be bound or practically foreclosed.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Rule 52.12(c) requires you to serve the motion on all parties affected, state the grounds, and attach a pleading setting forth the claim or defense for which intervention is sought. A motion with no attached proposed pleading gets denied.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199604

Rule 51.05(b): the application must be filed within 60 days from service of process or 30 days from the designation of the trial judge, whichever time is LONGER.
If the designation of the trial judge occurs less than 30 days before trial, the application must be filed prior to any appearance before the trial judge. An intervenor must file within 30 days of intervention or designation of the trial judge, whichever is later, and in no event may an intervening party obtain a change of judge unless the application is filed within 180 days of the designation of the trial judge.
Rule 51.05(b); transfer procedure Rule 51.05(e)

Application for change of judge (automatic, one per party)

ProceduralVerified

A peremptory application to disqualify the assigned judge without alleging or proving any cause. It must be granted if timely.

When to use it Early, when you want a different judge and do not need to prove bias.

If you miss it You lose the free change forever and are left with a change for cause or Rule 51.07, both of which require grounds.

Notes & source

Response / reply No response is contemplated — a timely application is granted as a matter of right.

Pro se note This is one of the most valuable rights a pro se litigant has and it evaporates fast. Diary BOTH triggers the day you are served and again the day a judge is assigned, then take the later date.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199581

No day count is stated in Rule 51.06 or Rule 51.07.
Rule 51.06(a): 'If a party requests and obtains either a change of venue or a change of judge, that party shall not be granted any additional change thereafter except for cause or under Rule 51.07.' Rule 51.07: if the judge is interested or related to any party, or shall have been counsel in the civil action, or is recused for any reason, the judge promptly shall transfer the case to the presiding judge for reassignment under Rule 51.05(e).
Rule 51.06(a)-(c); Rule 51.07

Change of judge or venue for cause; only one change rule

ProceduralVerified

Rule 51.06 provides that once you have obtained either a change of venue or a change of judge, you get no additional change except for cause or under Rule 51.07, and it sets the procedure when both are sought together. Rule 51.07 covers automatic disqualification.

When to use it When you have already used your peremptory change and still need relief, or when the judge is interested, related to a party, was counsel in the action, or is recused.

If you miss it No fixed forfeiture, but delay undercuts a cause showing.

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 51.06(c): the newly assigned judge decides the change of venue request; if venue is denied or changed within the same circuit, that judge stays on the case.

Pro se note Rule 51.06 was amended September 29, 2020, effective April 1, 2021 — do not rely on older form books for this one.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199582

Rule 51.03: the application must be filed within 10 days after the answer is due;
in condemnation cases, within 10 days after exceptions to the commissioners' report are filed; and in actions in which no answer is required, within 10 days of the return date of the summons or order to show cause.
Rule 51.03

Application for change of venue as a matter of right (small counties)

ProceduralVerified

In a civil action triable by jury pending in a county of 75,000 or fewer inhabitants, a change of venue from the inhabitants may be had as a matter of right, without alleging or proving any cause.

When to use it Jury-triable civil case in a small county where you do not want the local jury pool.

If you miss it The right is lost and you must proceed under Rule 51.04, which requires proving prejudice of the inhabitants.

Notes & source

Response / reply Copies must be served on all parties with notice of the time the application will be presented; the court allows suggestions from all parties before transferring to some other county convenient to the parties.

Pro se note The application need not be verified and may be signed by any party. Check the county's population first — over 75,000 and this rule does not apply.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199578

Rule 51.04: 'The application for change of venue must be filed at least thirty days before the trial date or within ten days after a trial date is fixed …
whichever date is later.'
Rule 51.04

Application for change of venue for cause (prejudice of the inhabitants)

ProceduralVerified

An application to move the case to another county because the inhabitants of this county are prejudiced against you, or the opposing party has undue influence over them.

When to use it Any county, when you can actually show local prejudice or undue influence.

If you miss it The application is untimely and denied; you try the case in a county you believe is hostile.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note The 'whichever date is later' language means a late-set trial date can revive your window — recalculate every time the court sets or resets trial.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199579

Rule 51.045: the motion to transfer venue must be filed within 60 days of service on the party seeking transfer.
A reply may be filed within 30 days after the filing of a motion to transfer for improper venue. If, after transfer, a party seeks a change of venue under Rule 51.03, the application must be filed within the later of (1) the time allowed by Rule 51.03, or (2) 10 days of being served with notice of the docketing.
Rule 51.045

Motion to transfer for improper venue

ProceduralVerified

A motion asserting the case was filed in a county where venue is not proper and asking that it be transferred to a county where venue is proper.

When to use it When the plaintiff filed in the wrong county under Missouri's venue statutes.

If you miss it Improper venue is not preserved and the case stays where it was filed.

Notes & source

Response / reply Reply within 30 days after the motion is filed; the reply must state the basis for venue in the forum or state reasons why venue is not proper.

Pro se note The motion must specify one or more counties in which you contend venue IS proper and state the basis for venue in each. A motion that only says 'venue is wrong here' is deficient.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199580

Same as any Rule 55.27 motion — within the time allowed for responding to the pleading (normally 30 days after service) …
or if no responsive pleading is permitted, within 30 days after service of the last pleading. Rule 55.27(a)(4), (a)(5).
Rule 55.27(a)(4) and (a)(5); waiver under Rule 55.27(g)(1); service governed by Rule 54

Motion to quash service / dismiss for insufficiency of process or service

ProceduralVerified

A challenge to how you were served or to the form of the summons, raised as a Rule 55.27(a)(4) or (a)(5) defense.

When to use it When you were never properly served, service was on the wrong person, or the summons is defective.

If you miss it Both defenses are WAIVED under Rule 55.27(g)(1) if not raised by motion or in your responsive pleading — and personal jurisdiction goes with them.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Do not file anything that asks the court for merits relief before you raise this. And raise it in the SAME motion as any other Rule 55.27 ground (Rule 55.27(f)).

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199658

No day count in the rule. Rule 74.05(a): upon a party's failure to plead or otherwise defend as provided by the rules …
an interlocutory order of default may be entered and a default judgment entered; an interlocutory order of default is not a prerequisite to entry of a default judgment.
Rule 74.05(a)-(c)

Motion for default judgment

DispositiveVerified

A plaintiff's motion for judgment because the defendant failed to plead or otherwise defend.

When to use it After the defendant's time to answer has run with nothing filed.

If you miss it Nothing forfeited by waiting, but the defendant may cure by filing an answer before default is taken.

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 74.05(b): any party may demand a jury to assess damages; if a jury is not demanded, the court shall assess any damages.

Pro se note You still must prove up your damages. A default admits liability, not the amount.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199745

Rule 74.05(d): the motion must be filed 'within a reasonable time not to exceed one year after the entry of the default judgment.' Both requirements apply …
reasonable time AND not more than one year.
Rule 74.05(d)

Motion to set aside default judgment

Post-judgmentVerified

A defaulted party's motion showing a meritorious defense and good cause, asking the court to undo the interlocutory order of default or the default judgment.

When to use it Any time you learn a default was taken against you, up to the outer limit.

If you miss it After one year this route is gone; you are left with Rule 74.06(b), whose grounds (4) void judgment and (5) satisfied/no longer equitable have no one-year cap but are much harder to establish.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule. The court may condition the setting aside upon such terms as are just, including payment of reasonable attorney fees and expenses incurred as a result of the default.

Pro se note Rule 74.05(d) defines 'good cause' to include a mistake or conduct that is not intentionally or recklessly designed to impede the judicial process — that is a forgiving standard, but you must ALSO plead facts constituting a meritorious defense. A 74.05(d) motion is treated as an independent action, not an after-trial motion under Rules 78.04/78.06/81.05.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199745

No deadline. RSMo 514.040.1: the court may act 'before or after the commencement of any suit pending before it.'
RSMo 514.040.1; incarcerated plaintiffs RSMo 514.040.2; legal-aid certification RSMo 514.040.3

Motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis (as a poor person)

ProceduralVerified

A motion asking the court to let you file and prosecute your case without paying filing fees and costs because you cannot afford them.

When to use it At or before filing suit, or any time costs are about to be assessed against you.

If you miss it Your filing may be refused or your case dismissed for nonpayment of the filing fee.

Notes & source

Response / reply RSMo 514.040.4: any party may present additional evidence on the financial condition of the parties.

Pro se note It is discretionary ('may, in its discretion') and the court may waive all or only part of the costs. The statute also lets the court assign you counsel. If judgment is entered for you, costs are recovered for the use of the officers of the court. If you are represented by a legal aid or law school clinic, RSMo 514.040.3 waives costs on a filed certification with no motion needed.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=514.040

Rule 52.02(d): the petition, consent and order of appointment must be filed before any proceedings are commenced.
Rule 52.02(g): if a minor defendant fails to procure the appointment of a guardian ad litem within one day after the time for appearance, the court shall appoint one.
Rule 52.02(b) (next friend for a minor); Rule 52.02(d); Rule 52.02(e); Rule 52.02(f); Rule 52.02(g)

Motion to appoint a guardian ad litem / next friend

ProceduralVerified

A motion to have the court appoint someone to sue or defend on behalf of a minor or an incapacitated party.

When to use it Whenever a party is a minor or is mentally or physically infirm.

If you miss it Rule 52.02(e): the action against a minor cannot proceed until a guardian or guardian ad litem is appointed, except discovery to determine minority status. A judgment taken without a required GAL is vulnerable.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Rule 52.02(f): the appointment is made on written request of the minor defendant if 14 or older, or of a relative or friend if under 14, with the proposed appointee's written consent.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199594

Safe harbor: the motion must be served on the opposing party first …
and 'shall not be filed with or presented to the court unless, within 30 days after service of the motion, the challenged claim, defense, request, demand, objection, contention, or denial is not withdrawn or appropriately corrected.' Rule 55.03(c). No outer deadline is set by the rule.
Rule 55.03(a) (signature required); Rule 55.03(b) (representations to the court); Rule 55.03(c) (sanctions); Rule 55.03(d) (does not apply to discovery — use Rule 61.01); Rule 55.03(e). Appearance/withdrawal of counsel moved to new Rule 55.035.

Motion for sanctions under Rule 55.03 (frivolous filings)

ProceduralVerified

A motion asking the court to sanction a party or lawyer for filing a paper for an improper purpose, or making legal contentions or factual allegations without support.

When to use it When the other side files something baseless or harassing and will not withdraw it.

If you miss it Filing the motion with the court before the 30-day safe harbor expires is itself grounds for denial.

Notes & source

Response / reply Court may also act on its own initiative by order to show cause. Not otherwise set by rule.

Pro se note Missouri's safe harbor is 30 days, NOT the 21 days of Federal Rule 11 — do not copy a federal form. The motion must be made separately from other motions or requests. Rule 55.03 was repealed and readopted by order of September 3, 2024, effective July 1, 2025, and Rule 55.035 (appearance and withdrawal of counsel) was created at the same time. Rule 55.03(a) requires a self-represented party to sign and give name, address, telephone and contact details.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=211115

Discovery 5

No day count is stated in Rule 61.01 for filing the motion.
The only deadline in the rule is in Rule 61.01(h): where the court assesses attorney fees for objections to approved discovery, if the fees are not paid within 60 days the court may strike pleadings or dismiss.
Rule 61.01(b) (interrogatories); 61.01(c) (requests for admission); 61.01(d) (production of documents); 61.01(f)-(g) (depositions); 61.01(h) (objections to approved discovery). Rule 55.03(d) confirms Rule 55.03 sanctions do NOT reach discovery.

Motion to compel discovery / for sanctions for failure to make discovery

DiscoveryVerified

A motion asking the court to order an opponent to answer interrogatories, produce documents, respond to requests for admission, or appear for deposition — and to sanction the failure.

When to use it After a discovery response is overdue, evasive, or incomplete.

If you miss it No forfeiture set by rule, but a motion filed on the eve of trial will usually be denied as too late to be useful, and you cannot complain on appeal about discovery you never moved to compel.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note IMPORTANT: Rule 61.01 itself contains NO good-faith conferral requirement — but most Missouri circuits impose one by LOCAL rule, and many judges will deny a motion to compel that lacks a certificate of conferral. Check your circuit's local rules and attach a conferral certificate anyway. Sanctions available include striking pleadings, dismissing the action, rendering default judgment, and awarding reasonable expenses including attorney fees. Rule 61.01 was amended March 2, 2021 (eff. Sept. 2, 2021) and November 23, 2021 (eff. July 1, 2022).

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199688

No day count in the rule. Rule 56.01(c) requires only a motion 'for good cause shown';
as a practical matter it must be filed before the response or the deposition is due.
Rule 56.01(c) (eight enumerated forms of protective order, including e-discovery); Rule 57.09(c) (non-party subpoena objections may be raised as a Rule 56.01(c) protective order)

Motion for protective order

DiscoveryVerified

A motion by a party, or by the non-party from whom discovery is sought, asking the court to limit or forbid discovery that is annoying, embarrassing, oppressive, or unduly burdensome or expensive.

When to use it When a discovery request or deposition notice is abusive, seeks trade secrets or private matter, or is disproportionate.

If you miss it If you simply do not respond you are exposed to Rule 61.01 sanctions; the protective order is the correct way to refuse.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule. Rule 56.01(c) also provides for expense awards and directs the court on how to evaluate undue-burden claims.

Pro se note The eight options in Rule 56.01(c) run from 'that the discovery not be had' to sealing and trade-secret protection — ask for the narrowest one that solves your problem, and file it BEFORE the response deadline runs. Rule 56.01 was amended effective July 1, 2019 (proportionality/e-discovery) and again March 2, 2021, effective September 2, 2021.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199668

Rule 57.09(b): the court may quash or modify the subpoena if it is unreasonable or oppressive — no day count stated.
For a NON-PARTY written objection to inspection and copying, the objection must be served 'within 10 days after service of the subpoena or before the time specified for compliance, whichever is earlier.'
Rule 57.09(b); Rule 57.09(c)

Motion to quash or modify a deposition subpoena

DiscoveryVerified

A motion, usually by the subpoenaed person, asking the court to kill or narrow a subpoena for a deposition or for documents.

When to use it When a subpoena is unreasonable or oppressive, or a non-party is asked to produce documents it should not have to.

If you miss it A late objection is waived and the subpoenaing party may inspect and copy without a court order.

Notes & source

Response / reply If a timely and specific objection is made, the party who issued and served the subpoena is not entitled to inspect or copy the subpoenaed items except pursuant to an order of the court.

Pro se note 'Whichever is EARLIER' — if the subpoena says produce in 5 days, you have 5 days, not 10. Read the compliance date first.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199678

Notice of deposition: 'not less than seven days notice in writing to every other party to the action and to a non-party deponent' (Rule 57.03(b)).
The motion to terminate or limit may be made 'at any time during the taking of the deposition.'
Rule 57.03(b) (7 days notice); Rule 57.03 (objections noted by the officer; motion to cease examination)

Motion to terminate or limit a deposition; deposition notice

DiscoveryVerified

A motion made during a deposition, by a party or the deponent, to stop or restrict an examination being conducted in bad faith or so as unreasonably to annoy, embarrass or oppress.

When to use it Mid-deposition, when questioning becomes abusive.

If you miss it Abusive questioning continues; objections not noted may be waived.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note You cannot simply walk out. Rule 57.03 requires you to move — suspend the deposition and seek the order. Rule 57.03 was amended through July 1, 2022 (videoconferencing and duration limits).

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199672

Rule 44.01(b): the court may, for cause shown, order the period enlarged if request is made BEFORE the expiration of the period originally prescribed;
if request is made AFTER the period has expired, the court may permit the act where the failure to act was the result of excusable neglect.
Rule 44.01(b); local case-management rules

Motion to extend discovery deadlines / amend the scheduling order

DiscoveryVerified

A motion asking the court to enlarge a discovery cutoff or other case-management date it previously set.

When to use it When you cannot complete discovery within the court's schedule.

If you miss it You must show excusable neglect rather than mere cause — a materially harder standard.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Always file BEFORE the deadline runs. 'Cause shown' before expiration is far easier than 'excusable neglect' after. Note that Rule 44.01(b) forbids the court from extending certain deadlines at all — see the time-computation entry.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199564

Pretrial 9

A claimant may move at any time after the expiration of 30 days from the commencement of the action or after service of a motion for summary judgment by the adverse party.
A defending party may move at any time. Rule 74.04(a)-(b).
Rule 74.04(a), (b), (c); Rule 74.04(c)(1) motion contents; (c)(2) response; (c)(3) reply; (c)(4) sur-reply

Motion for summary judgment

DispositiveVerified

A motion asserting there is no genuine dispute as to any material fact and the movant is entitled to judgment as a matter of law, supported by a statement of uncontroverted material facts in separately numbered paragraphs with specific record citations.

When to use it After the facts are developed and you can show the material ones are not genuinely disputed.

If you miss it Fatal. Rule 74.04(c)(2): a failure to properly respond to a numbered paragraph — with specific references to the discovery, exhibits or affidavits — 'shall constitute an admission of the truth of that numbered paragraph.' Denials resting on the allegations of your own pleadings do not count.

Notes & source

Response / reply Response: within 30 days after the motion is served, the adverse party shall serve a response on all parties. Reply: within 15 days after service of the response, the movant may file a reply memorandum of law and respond to any additional material facts. Sur-reply: within 15 days of service, if the movant's reply submitted additional uncontroverted material facts, the responding party may file a sur-reply addressing those facts. 'After the response, reply and any sur-reply have been filed or the deadlines therefor have expired, the court shall decide the motion.'

Pro se note This is where most pro se cases are lost, and it is lost procedurally, not on the merits. You must answer EVERY numbered paragraph, one at a time, admitting or denying, and every denial must cite actual record evidence — a deposition page, an exhibit, or your own sworn affidavit. Attach the evidence. Also file a separate legal memorandum; the movant is required to file one and so should you. Yes, a sur-reply IS allowed, but only if the movant's reply added new uncontroverted facts.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199744

No fixed day count for filing. Rule 65.03 …
the motion must be in writing and accompanied by the affidavit of the movant or some other credible person setting forth the facts upon which the motion is based, unless the adverse party consents that the motion may be made orally. In any motion for continuance made WITHIN 30 DAYS of the date the matter is scheduled to be heard, the lawyer shall certify that the party for whose benefit the motion is filed has been consulted, is aware of the contents of the motion, and the party's position on it.
Rule 65.03; Rule 65.04 (continuance on account of absence of witnesses)

Motion for continuance

ProceduralVerified

A motion to postpone a trial or hearing.

When to use it When you cannot be ready — illness, an unavailable material witness, a late-arising conflict.

If you miss it The case is tried or heard without you or without your evidence; an oral request with no affidavit and no consent is procedurally defective.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note The affidavit is not optional. Rule 65.04 tells you exactly what an absent-witness affidavit must show: (1) materiality of the evidence and the movant's due diligence; (2) the witness's name and residence if known, or the efforts made to find them, plus reasonable grounds to believe the attendance or testimony will be procured within a reasonable time; (3) what particular facts the movant believes the witness will prove and that no other person's evidence could have been procured; and (4) that the witness is not absent by the movant's connivance, consent or procurement, and that the motion is made in good faith and not for vexation or delay. Rules 65.03 and 65.04 were both amended March 5, 2024, effective January 1, 2025.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199699

UNVERIFIED — no Missouri Supreme Court Rule sets a deadline for a motion in limine.
Timing is controlled by the trial court's scheduling order and by local rules, which vary by circuit.
No Supreme Court Rule; trial court's inherent authority and local/case-management rules

Motion in limine

Evidentiary / trialUnverified

A pretrial motion asking the court to rule in advance that certain evidence may not be mentioned or offered at trial.

When to use it Before trial, to keep prejudicial evidence away from the jury.

If you miss it Missouri law treats a ruling on a motion in limine as interlocutory and NOT preserving anything. You must still object at trial when the evidence is offered, or the point is not preserved for appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by any rule.

Pro se note Winning a motion in limine is not enough. Object again, on the record, at the moment the evidence comes in. Check your circuit's local rules for the filing deadline — commonly set in the trial-setting order.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199564

No deadline stated. Rule 66.01(b): 'When civil actions involving a common question of law or fact are pending before the court, it may order a joint hearing or trial...
it may order all the civil actions consolidated.'
Rule 66.01(a) (same parties, liquidated demands); 66.01(b) (common question); 66.01(c) (family court); 66.01(d) (injury to spouse); 66.01(e) (notice by first class mail)

Motion to consolidate

ProceduralVerified

A motion to try two or more pending civil actions together because they involve a common question of law or fact.

When to use it When you and the other side are litigating overlapping cases in the same court.

If you miss it Duplicative litigation and inconsistent results; no forfeiture.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Consolidation is discretionary ('may'). Frame it around cost and delay avoidance, which is the language Rule 66.01(b) itself uses.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199704

No deadline stated in Rule 66.02.
Rule 66.02

Motion for separate trial (severance)

ProceduralVerified

A motion to try one claim, cross-claim, counterclaim, third-party claim, or issue separately from the rest.

When to use it To avoid prejudice, or when one issue could dispose of the case.

If you miss it One trial on everything; no forfeiture.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Pair this with a motion in limine when the prejudicial material is confined to one claim.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199705

A TRO granted WITHOUT notice 'shall expire within such time after entry …
not to exceed ten days,' as the court fixes, unless within that time it is extended for a like period (up to 10 days) after notice to the party against whom the order is sought, or unless the party against whom it is directed consents to a longer extension. Rule 92.02(b). If a TRO is in effect for more than 30 days without a hearing on an application for preliminary injunction, the court shall schedule a hearing at the earliest possible date.
Rule 92.02(a) (showing required); Rule 92.02(b)(1) (without notice); Rule 92.02(c) (bond)

Motion for temporary restraining order

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

An emergency order, sometimes issued without notice, restraining conduct until a preliminary injunction can be heard.

When to use it When irreparable harm will occur before a noticed hearing can be held.

If you miss it The TRO lapses by operation of the rule when the 10 days run — it does not silently continue.

Notes & source

Response / reply The party against whom the TRO issued may move to dissolve or modify it.

Pro se note For a TRO without notice you must file a VERIFIED petition or an affidavit reciting specific facts, and you must show both the Rule 92.02(a) grounds AND that notice cannot be given or would defeat the purpose of the order. A bond with sufficient surety is required before a TRO or injunction issues (except suits by the state in its own behalf) — plan for that cost.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199919

No day count for filing. Rule 92.02 requires notice and a hearing;
the 30-day trigger in Rule 92.02(b) forces the court to set the preliminary injunction hearing at the earliest possible date when a TRO has been in effect more than 30 days without one.
Rule 92.02(a), (b), (c)

Motion for preliminary injunction

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

A motion for an injunction to hold the status quo until the case is tried on the merits, granted only after notice and a hearing.

When to use it When you need relief for the life of the case, not just 10 days.

If you miss it Only the short-lived TRO protects you, and it expires by rule.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Bond is required here too. Ask the court to set a nominal bond and explain your finances if you cannot post a large one.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199919

UNVERIFIED — Missouri has no civil procedure rule in Rules 41-101 setting a deadline or procedure for judicial notice. It is governed by statute and common law.
No Missouri Supreme Court civil rule located; governed by Missouri evidence statutes and case law

Request for judicial notice

Evidentiary / trialUnverified

A request that the court accept a fact or a law as established without formal proof.

When to use it For court records, statutes, ordinances, and indisputable facts.

If you miss it You must formally prove the fact.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Raise it in writing before trial and again when the evidence matters. Do not assume the judge will take notice of your own prior case file without being asked on the record.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199564

Rule 67.02(a): a plaintiff may dismiss without order of court (1) …
prior to the swearing of the jury panel for the voir dire examination, or (2) in cases tried without a jury, prior to the introduction of evidence at the trial.
Rule 67.02

Voluntary dismissal (plaintiff)

DispositiveVerified

A plaintiff's dismissal of the plaintiff's own civil action, which in defined windows may be taken without any court order.

When to use it When you want out of your own case — but understand the one-refiling consequence.

If you miss it After those points you need a stipulation signed by the adverse party or a court order stating the grounds.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not applicable.

Pro se note Dismissal is without prejudice unless you say otherwise, BUT if you previously dismissed the same claim, a second dismissal without prejudice requires a stipulation or a court order. Also, if you dismiss within 10 days of trial and refile, the court may order you to pay the defendant's unpaid costs and witness expenses for the second trial. Watch your statute of limitations before dismissing anything.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199708

Trial 2

Rule 72.01(a): 'A party may move for a directed verdict at the close of the evidence offered by an opponent.' The motion must state the specific grounds therefor.
A motion for directed verdict which is not granted is not a waiver of trial by jury even though all parties have moved for directed verdicts, and the movant does not waive the right to offer evidence by making the motion.
Rule 72.01(a)

Motion for directed verdict

DispositiveVerified

A motion at trial arguing the opponent's evidence is legally insufficient to support a verdict, so the court should take the case from the jury.

When to use it At the close of the opponent's evidence, and again at the close of all the evidence.

If you miss it This is the classic preservation trap: without a directed verdict motion at the close of ALL the evidence you cannot move for judgment notwithstanding the verdict under Rule 72.01(b), and sufficiency of the evidence is not preserved for appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Make it twice — at the close of the plaintiff's case and again at the close of all evidence — and state your specific grounds out loud on the record each time. 'I move for a directed verdict' with no grounds preserves nothing. Rule 44.01(b) forbids the court from extending Rule 72.01 deadlines.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199737

UNVERIFIED — no Missouri Supreme Court Rule in Rules 41-101 sets a deadline or procedure for a motion for mistrial.
Missouri case law requires it be made immediately, at the time of the incident.
No Supreme Court Rule located; trial court's inherent authority; preservation via Rule 78.07

Motion for mistrial

Evidentiary / trialUnverified

A request to abort the trial because something has happened that cannot be cured by an instruction to disregard.

When to use it Immediately when incurable prejudice occurs — improper argument, inadmissible evidence blurted out, juror misconduct.

If you miss it Waiver. A mistrial request made after the verdict is too late.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Object first, ask the court to instruct the jury to disregard, and only then request a mistrial — Missouri courts call mistrial a drastic remedy and want to see you asked for the lesser relief first. Then carry the point into your Rule 78.07 after-trial motion.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200293

Post-trial 4

Rule 72.01(b): 'Not later than thirty days after entry of judgment' a party who has moved for a directed verdict at the close of all the evidence may move to …
have the verdict and any judgment entered thereon set aside and to have judgment entered in accordance with the motion for directed verdict; if no verdict was returned, such a party may move for judgment within 30 days after the jury has been discharged.
Rule 72.01(b); Rule 78.06 (deemed overruled at 90 days); Rule 81.05(a)(2)

Motion for judgment notwithstanding the verdict (JNOV)

Post-judgmentNo extensionVerified

A post-verdict motion asking the court to enter judgment for the party who moved for a directed verdict, notwithstanding what the jury did.

When to use it After an adverse verdict, when the evidence was legally insufficient.

If you miss it The 30 days CANNOT be extended — Rule 44.01(b) expressly forbids the court from enlarging time for action under Rule 72.01. Miss it and the JNOV is gone.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note A JNOV motion is an authorized after-trial motion, so filing it also extends the time for appeal under Rule 81.05(a)(2). It is dead on arrival unless you moved for a directed verdict at the close of ALL the evidence.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199737

Rule 78.04: 'Any motion for new trial and any motion to amend the judgment or opinion shall be filed not later than thirty days after the entry of judgment.' A …
prematurely filed motion is treated as filed when the judgment becomes final.
Rule 78.04; contents and preservation Rule 78.07; deemed overruled Rule 78.06; effect on appeal time Rule 81.05(a)(2)

Motion for new trial

Post-judgmentNo extensionVerified

An after-trial motion asking the court to set aside the judgment and try the case again, specifying each allegation of error.

When to use it After judgment, to correct trial errors and to preserve them for appeal.

If you miss it The 30 days CANNOT be extended — Rule 44.01(b) expressly forbids enlarging time for action under Rule 78.04. Errors not raised are generally not preserved for appellate review.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note This is the single most important deadline after judgment. It is jurisdictional in effect and no judge can give you more time. Rule 78.06 then gives the trial court 90 days: 'Any motion for new trial, motion to amend the judgment or opinion, or motion for judgment notwithstanding the verdict is overruled for all purposes if the trial court does not rule on it within ninety days after the date the last such timely motion is filed.' In computing the 90 days, no day is counted during which the court lacks power to act because of an order of a superior state court or because of removal of the action.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200290

Same 30 days as Rule 78.04. Rule 78.07(c) has no separate deadline — it tells you WHERE the complaint must be raised.
Rule 78.07(c); Rule 78.04

After-trial motion — allegations of error required (preservation)

Post-judgmentVerified

The rule governing what an after-trial motion must actually say in order to preserve a point for appeal, including the special requirement for complaints about the judgment itself.

When to use it Whenever you draft a motion for new trial or to amend the judgment.

If you miss it Rule 78.07(c): 'In all cases, allegations of error relating to the form or language of the judgment, including the failure to make statutorily required findings, must be raised in a motion to amend the judgment in order to be preserved for appellate review.' Skip it and the appellate court will not reach the point, however good it is.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note If your complaint is that the judgment is ambiguous, incomplete, internally inconsistent, or omits findings a statute requires, a motion for new trial is NOT the right vehicle — you need a motion to AMEND the judgment. Many pro se appeals die here. Rule 78.07 was last amended December 27, 2016, effective July 1, 2017.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200293

Rule 78.04: 'not later than thirty days after the entry of judgment.'
Rule 78.04; Rule 78.07(c); Rule 78.06 (90-day deemed overruling); Rule 75.01

Motion to amend the judgment

Post-judgmentNo extensionVerified

An after-trial motion asking the trial court to change the judgment's terms, form, or language, or to add required findings.

When to use it When the judgment is wrong in form, incomplete, or omits statutorily required findings.

If you miss it Cannot be extended (Rule 44.01(b) names Rule 78.04). Form-and-language complaints are waived on appeal (Rule 78.07(c)).

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule. Deemed overruled if not ruled within 90 days after the last timely after-trial motion is filed (Rule 78.06).

Pro se note This is an authorized after-trial motion, so it also resets the appeal clock under Rule 81.05(a)(2).

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200290

Post-judgment 5

Rule 75.01 (as adopted by order of October 1, 2024, effective July 1, 2025) …
'Except as otherwise provided in Rule 81.045, the trial court retains control over judgments during the 30-day period after entry of judgment and may, after giving the parties an opportunity to be heard and for good cause, vacate, reopen, correct, amend, or modify its judgment within that time. Not later than 30 days after entry of judgment, the court of its own initiative may order a new trial for any reason for which it might have granted a new trial on motion of a party, and every order granting a new trial shall specify the grounds therefor.'
Rule 75.01; Rule 81.045

Trial court's 30-day control over the judgment (Rule 75.01)

Post-judgmentVerified

The window during which the trial court keeps power over its own judgment and can change it, with or without a motion.

When to use it Understand it whenever you are deciding how fast to move after judgment.

If you miss it After 30 days (and after any authorized after-trial motion is spent) the trial court loses jurisdiction over the judgment; your only routes are Rule 74.06, Rule 74.05(d), or appeal. Rule 44.01(b) forbids extending Rule 75.01.

Notes & source

Response / reply 'The 30-day period after entry of judgment for granting a new trial of the court's own initiative is not shortened by the filing of a notice of appeal but is terminated when the record on appeal is filed in the appellate court or an order shortening this period is entered under Rule 81.045.'

Pro se note Rule 75.01 was rewritten effective July 1, 2025 — older treatises and forms do not reflect the Rule 81.045 carve-out. Also note: after a notice of appeal is filed but before the record on appeal is filed, the trial court may still vacate, amend or modify upon STIPULATION of the parties accompanied by a withdrawal of the appeal.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=213129

Rule 74.06(c): 'The motion shall be made within a reasonable time and for reasons (1) …
and (2) and (3) of subdivision (b) not more than one year after the judgment or order was entered.' So grounds (1) mistake/inadvertence/surprise/excusable neglect, (2) fraud/misrepresentation/other misconduct of an adverse party, and (3) the judgment is irregular carry a hard ONE-YEAR outer limit. Grounds (4) the judgment is void and (5) the judgment has been satisfied, released or discharged or it is no longer equitable are limited only by 'reasonable time.' Clerical mistakes under Rule 74.06(a) may be corrected at any time.
Rule 74.06(a), (b)(1)-(5), (c), (d)

Motion for relief from judgment or order

Post-judgmentNo extensionVerified

A motion attacking a judgment after the trial court's ordinary control has expired, on specific enumerated grounds.

When to use it When you discover mistake, excusable neglect, fraud, an irregular judgment, a void judgment, or that the judgment has been satisfied or is no longer equitable.

If you miss it Grounds (1)-(3) are absolutely barred after one year. Rule 44.01(b) does not list Rule 74.06 among the rules a court may not extend, but the one-year limit is in Rule 74.06(c) itself and is not treated as extendable.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule. A Rule 74.06(b) motion does not affect the finality of the judgment or suspend its operation.

Pro se note Because a Rule 74.06 motion does NOT suspend the judgment, the other side can still execute on it while your motion is pending — consider a stay. Rule 74.06(d) preserves an INDEPENDENT ACTION to relieve a party from a judgment or to set aside a judgment for fraud upon the court, and abolishes coram nobis, coram vobis, audita querela and bills of review. 'Fraud upon the court' via independent action has no one-year cap.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199746

Rule 74.09(a): a motion for revival must be filed by a judgment creditor 'within ten years after entry of the judgment …
the last payment of record, or the last prior revival of the judgment.'
Rule 74.09; service under Rule 54

Motion to revive a judgment

Post-judgmentVerified

A judgment creditor's motion to revive a judgment so it remains enforceable.

When to use it Before a money judgment goes dormant.

If you miss it The judgment cannot be revived and becomes unenforceable.

Notes & source

Response / reply Upon filing, an order shall issue to the judgment debtor to show cause on a day certain why the judgment should not be revived. If the debtor fails to appear and show cause, the court shall enter an order reviving the judgment.

Pro se note If you are the DEBTOR and get a show cause order, appear on the day certain — non-appearance means automatic revival. Rule 74.09 was amended June 25, 2019, effective January 1, 2020.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199749

UNVERIFIED — no general Missouri Supreme Court Rule fixes a deadline for a post-judgment motion for attorney's fees in civil cases.
Costs are governed by Rule 77. Where fees are authorized by a specific statute, that statute's timing controls.
Rule 77 (costs) — text not read this session; fee entitlement is statutory or contractual. Sunshine Law fees: RSMo 610.027.3 and .4 (verified).

Motion for attorney's fees and costs

Post-judgmentUnverified

A motion to have fees and/or costs taxed against the losing party.

When to use it After you prevail, where a contract or statute authorizes fees.

If you miss it Fees may be treated as waived, or a late request may be outside the court's jurisdiction once the judgment is final under Rule 75.01/81.05.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by any rule read this session.

Pro se note Missouri follows the American Rule — no fees unless a statute or contract says so. Safest practice is to plead the fee request in your petition and raise it before the judgment becomes final, so the trial court still has jurisdiction. Do not rely on this entry's timing; check the specific fee statute you are invoking.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=610.027

UNVERIFIED — I did not read the text of Rule 76 (Executions), Rule 90 (Garnishment), or Missouri's civil contempt statutes this session, and cannot state their deadlines.
Rule 74.09's ten-year revival limit (verified) does bound how long a money judgment stays enforceable.
Rule 76 (executions) and Rule 90 (garnishment) — NOT verified this session; Rule 76.28 (discovery in aid of execution) exists but text not read; Rule 74.09 (verified)

Motion to enforce judgment / for contempt

Post-judgmentUnverified

A motion to make the losing party comply, by execution and garnishment for money judgments or by civil contempt for orders directing conduct.

When to use it When the judgment debtor will not pay or will not obey.

If you miss it A dormant judgment cannot be executed on until revived under Rule 74.09.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not verified.

Pro se note Treat this entry as a pointer, not authority. Read Rule 76 and Rule 90 directly before filing. Note Rule 76.28 allows discovery in aid of execution to find the debtor's assets.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199749

Time computation 4

Rule 44.01(a): 'In computing any period of time prescribed or allowed by these rules …
by order of court, or by any applicable statute, the day of the act, event, or default after which the designated period of time begins to run is not to be included.' The last day of the period IS included, unless it is a Saturday, Sunday or legal holiday, in which case the period runs until the end of the next day that is not a Saturday, Sunday or legal holiday. When the period of time prescribed or allowed is LESS THAN SEVEN DAYS, intermediate Saturdays, Sundays and legal holidays are EXCLUDED from the computation.
Rule 44.01(a)

Computing deadlines (Rule 44.01(a))

Time computationVerified

The rule for counting days under the Missouri rules, court orders, and statutes.

When to use it Every single time you calculate a deadline.

If you miss it Miscounting is not excusable neglect in most cases; the deadline is the deadline.

Notes & source

Pro se note Start counting the DAY AFTER the triggering event. For any period of 7 days or more, count every calendar day including weekends — only the LAST day rolls forward if it lands on a weekend or holiday. Under 7 days, skip weekends and holidays entirely. Rule 44.01 was repealed and readopted by order of May 1, 2024, effective January 1, 2025 (the same order repealed Rule 47, Terms of Court).

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199564

Rule 44.01(b): the court for cause shown may, at any time in its discretion, (1) …
with or without motion or notice order the period enlarged if request therefor is made BEFORE the expiration of the period originally prescribed, or (2) upon motion made after the expiration of the specified period permit the act to be done where the failure to act was the result of EXCUSABLE NEGLECT. BUT the court may NOT extend the time for taking any action under Rules 52.13, 72.01, 73.01, 75.01, 78.04, 81.04, 81.07 and 84.035, nor the time for commencing civil actions.
Rule 44.01(b)

Enlargement of time — and the deadlines a court CANNOT extend

Time computationNo extensionVerified

The court's power to extend deadlines, and the express list of deadlines it has no power to extend.

When to use it Before you assume you can ask for more time.

If you miss it No relief at all for the non-extendable deadlines — no motion, no excusable neglect, no exception.

Notes & source

Pro se note Memorize this list. In plain terms the court CANNOT give you more time for: substitution of parties (52.13), directed verdict / JNOV (72.01), court-tried case findings (73.01), the 30-day control period (75.01), the motion for new trial or to amend the judgment (78.04), the notice of appeal (81.04), the six-month late-appeal special order (81.07), and 84.035 — or for filing suit at all. Everything on that list is a hard wall. Note that Rule 74.06 is NOT on this list, but Rule 74.06(c)'s own one-year limit still governs.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199564

Rule 44.01(d): when service is made by MAIL, three days are added to the prescribed response period.
Rule 103.08 (e-filing service): 'Service by the electronic filing system is complete upon transmission except that, for the purposes of calculating the time for filing a response, a transmission made on a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday, or after 5:00 p.m., shall be considered complete on the next day that is not a Saturday, Sunday, or a legal holiday.' NO additional days are added for electronic service.
Rule 44.01(d); Rule 103.08

Three extra days for service by mail — and NOT for e-service

Time computationVerified

The mailbox rule adding days to a response period, and the separate electronic-service timing rule.

When to use it Whenever you compute a response deadline triggered by service of a paper.

If you miss it Adding three days to an e-served deadline that does not get them will make you late.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is a very common and very costly error. If you were served through the e-filing system you get ZERO extra days — only the 5:00 p.m./weekend shift in Rule 103.08. The three days in Rule 44.01(d) are for paper mail only. Rule 103.08 was amended December 31, 2019, effective July 1, 2020.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200015

Rule 44.01(c): a written motion, other than one which may be heard ex parte …
and notice of the hearing thereof shall be served 'not later than five days before the time specified for the hearing,' unless a different period is fixed by these rules or by order of the court. When a motion is supported by affidavit, the affidavit shall be served with the motion; opposing affidavits may be served not later than one day before the hearing, unless the court permits them to be served at some other time.
Rule 44.01(c)

Motion notice and supporting affidavits (Rule 44.01(c))

Time computationVerified

The default notice period for a written motion set for hearing, and when affidavits must be served.

When to use it Every time you notice a motion for hearing.

If you miss it The hearing may be continued or the motion denied for insufficient notice.

Notes & source

Response / reply Opposing affidavits not later than one day before the hearing.

Pro se note Because 5 days is less than 7, Rule 44.01(a) means you EXCLUDE weekends and holidays when counting it — 5 days notice is really about a full week on the calendar.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199564

Special track — Adult Abuse Act 2

RSMo 455.040.1(1): 'Not later than fifteen days after the filing of a petition that meets the requirements of section 455.020 …
a hearing shall be held unless the court deems, for good cause shown, that a continuance should be granted.'
RSMo 455.040.1(1); RSMo 455.020; duration RSMo 455.040.1(1); renewal RSMo 455.040.1(2)-(3); modification RSMo 455.060.1; penalties RSMo 455.085.7-.8

Petition for order of protection; ex parte and full orders

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

A civil petition under the Adult Abuse Act for an ex parte order of protection and then, after a hearing, a full order of protection against domestic violence, stalking, or sexual assault.

When to use it When you need immediate court protection from abuse, stalking, or sexual assault.

If you miss it Failing to appear at the 15-day hearing normally means the ex parte order dissolves and the petition is dismissed.

Notes & source

Response / reply At the hearing, if the petitioner proves the allegation of domestic violence, stalking, or sexual assault by a preponderance of the evidence and the respondent cannot show the alleged acts were otherwise justified under the law, the court shall issue a full order of protection.

Pro se note DURATION: unless the court makes specific written findings after an evidentiary hearing that the respondent poses a serious danger to the physical or mental health of the petitioner or of a minor household member, a full order is valid for at least 180 days and not more than one year. With those serious-danger findings, it is valid for at least two years and not more than ten years. RENEWAL: on motion of the petitioner and after a hearing, the full order may be renewed annually; a subsequent act of abuse is NOT required for renewal. AUTOMATIC RENEWAL: under 455.040.1(3) the court may order automatic renewal unless the respondent requests a hearing by 30 days prior to expiration. MODIFICATION: RSMo 455.060.1 — after notice and hearing, on motion with an affidavit showing a change in circumstances sufficient to warrant modification; all full orders are FINAL and APPEALABLE. If the court found serious danger, RSMo 455.040.1(5) bars modification for at least two years and only on proof of treatment and rehabilitation.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.040

Not a deadline. RSMo 455.085.8: violation of the specified terms and conditions of a FULL order of protection of which the respondent has notice is a CLASS A …
MISDEMEANOR, 'unless the respondent has previously pleaded guilty to or has been found guilty in any division of the circuit court of violating an ex parte order of protection or a full order of protection within five years of the date of the subsequent violation, in which case the subsequent violation shall be a class E felony.' RSMo 455.085.7 sets the same class A misdemeanor / class E felony structure for violation of an EX PARTE order.
RSMo 455.085.7; RSMo 455.085.8; arrest RSMo 455.085.2

Consequence of violating an order of protection

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

The criminal penalty for violating an ex parte or full order of protection.

When to use it Know this whether you hold the order or are subject to one.

If you miss it Criminal liability for the respondent.

Notes & source

Pro se note Notice does not require formal service: under 455.085.8 a party is deemed to have notice if a responding officer presented a copy of the order to the respondent, or if notice was given by actual communication in a manner reasonably likely to advise the respondent. Under 455.085.2 an officer with probable cause to believe a protected-order violation occurred SHALL arrest, whether or not it happened in the officer's presence. Note RSMo 455.085.10: nothing in the section creates a private cause of action for damages.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=455.085

Special track — Small claims 1

RSMo 482.365.2: 'The right to trial de novo shall be perfected by filing an application for trial de novo with the clerk of the small claims court within ten …
days after the judgment is rendered.' The clerk mails or serves a copy on the opposing party within fifteen days after the judgment was rendered.
RSMo 482.365.2; small claims procedure RSMo 482.310

Application for trial de novo from small claims court

AppellateVerified

A do-over: a completely new trial in the circuit court after a small claims judgment.

When to use it When you lose in small claims and want a real trial.

If you miss it The small claims judgment stands and becomes enforceable. Ten days is absolute.

Notes & source

Response / reply No application for trial de novo stays execution unless the applicant, with one or more solvent sureties approved by the small claims court judge, enters into a recognizance within the time prescribed for filing the application.

Pro se note Two separate 10-day acts: file the application AND, if you want to stop collection, post the recognizance within the same 10 days. Judgment by CONSENT cannot be taken de novo. In the trial de novo the practice is that of trials before circuit judges — the informal small claims rules no longer apply, and by agreement of the parties a jury of not less than six may be had. If the defendant applies for trial de novo, the plaintiff may amend the petition up to the circuit court's jurisdictional limit. Note: RSMo 482.310(1) lets you appear without a lawyer in small claims itself, but a 2013 case (Palmore v. City of Pacific) held that FILING an application for trial de novo constitutes the practice of law — a real trap for a non-lawyer filing on behalf of an entity.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=482.365

Special track — Associate circuit 1

RSMo 512.190.1: 'The right of trial de novo provided in subsection 1 of section 512.180 shall be perfected by filing an application for trial de novo with the …
clerk serving the associate circuit judge within ten days after the judgment is rendered.' A copy is mailed or served by the clerk within fifteen days after the judgment was rendered.
RSMo 512.180.1; RSMo 512.190.1; RSMo 512.190.2

Application for trial de novo from an associate circuit judge

AppellateVerified

A new trial in circuit court after a judgment by an associate circuit judge in the categories where trial de novo, rather than appeal on the record, is the remedy.

When to use it After a civil bench judgment tried before an associate circuit judge in municipal court, or under chapter 482 (small claims) or chapter 535 (landlord-tenant / rent and possession).

If you miss it You lose the trial de novo. Ten days.

Notes & source

Response / reply No stay of execution unless the applicant, with one or more solvent sureties approved by the associate circuit judge, enters into a recognizance within the time prescribed in the first sentence of RSMo 512.190.1.

Pro se note Know which track you are on. RSMo 512.180.2: in ALL OTHER contested civil cases before an associate circuit judge a record is kept and the remedy is an APPEAL ON THE RECORD to the appellate court under Rules 81/84, not a trial de novo. Rent-and-possession and small claims get trial de novo; most other associate circuit civil cases do not. RSMo 512.190.2: appeals to the court of appeals or Supreme Court are governed by the same rules applicable to appeals from circuit judges.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=512.190

Special track — Sunshine Law 1

RSMo 610.027.5: 'Suit for enforcement shall be brought within one year from which the violation is ascertainable and in no event shall it be brought later than …
two years after the violation.'
RSMo 610.027.1 (who may sue and where); 610.027.2 (burden); 610.027.3 (knowing violation); 610.027.4 (purposeful violation); 610.027.5 (limitations and voiding action)

Suit to enforce the Sunshine Law

DispositiveVerified

A civil action by an aggrieved person, taxpayer, citizen, the attorney general or a prosecuting attorney to enforce Missouri's open meetings and open records law.

When to use it When a public governmental body has closed a meeting, record or vote it should not have, or refuses a records request.

If you miss it The claim is time-barred; the outer two-year limit is absolute regardless of when you discovered it.

Notes & source

Response / reply RSMo 610.027.2: once the party seeking enforcement shows the body is subject to the law and held a closed meeting, record or vote, the BURDEN OF PERSUASION SHIFTS to the body and its members to demonstrate compliance.

Pro se note Sue in the circuit court for the county where the public governmental body has its principal place of business. The burden-shifting in 610.027.2 is the most valuable feature for a self-represented litigant. FEES: for a KNOWING violation the court 'may order' payment of all costs and reasonable attorney fees plus a civil penalty up to $1,000; for a PURPOSEFUL violation the court 'SHALL order' costs and reasonable attorney fees plus a penalty up to $5,000. Under 610.027.1, once you serve suit the custodian may not transfer, alter, destroy or dispose of the record until the court directs otherwise.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=610.027

Missouri appellate 14

Appellate 14

Rule 81.05(a): 'Except as otherwise provide[d] in Rule 81.045, for the purpose of ascertaining the time within which an appeal may be taken …
(1) A judgment becomes final in a civil matter at the expiration of 30 days after its entry if no timely authorized after-trial motion is filed. (2) If a party timely files an authorized after-trial motion, the judgment becomes final at the earlier of the following: (A) 90 days from the date the last timely motion was filed, on which date all motions not ruled shall be deemed overruled; or (B) If all motions have been ruled, then the date of ruling of the last motion to be ruled or 30 days after entry of judgment, whichever is later.'
Rule 81.05(a)(1)-(3), (b), (c); Rule 78.06; Rule 81.045

When a judgment becomes final for purposes of appeal

AppellateNo extensionVerified

The rule that fixes the date from which the notice-of-appeal clock runs. Not a motion, but the calculation everything appellate depends on.

When to use it Immediately after judgment, to compute your appeal deadline.

If you miss it Miscount this and you miss the notice of appeal, which cannot be extended.

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 81.05(b): a prematurely filed notice of appeal 'shall be considered as filed immediately after the time the judgment becomes final for the purpose of appeal.' Rule 81.05(c): the 90-day period is computed as in Rule 78.06.

Pro se note Rule 81.05 was repealed and readopted by order of October 1, 2024, effective July 1, 2025. Rule 81.05(a)(3) adds that filing and disposition of such motions has the same effect on time for appeal in all cases whether or not the motion has any function other than to seek relief in the trial court. Because a premature notice of appeal is SAVED by 81.05(b), when in doubt file the notice of appeal early rather than late.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200308

Rule 81.04(a): 'No such appeal shall be effective unless the notice of appeal shall be filed not later than ten days after the judgment …
decree, or order appealed from becomes final.' Cross appeal, Rule 81.04(c): 'If a timely notice of appeal is filed by a party, any other party may file a notice of appeal within ten days of the date the first notice of appeal was filed.'
Rule 81.04(a) (10 days); 81.04(b) (style of case); 81.04(c) (cross appeals); 81.04(d) (docket fee)

Notice of appeal

AppellateVerified

The document filed with the CLERK OF THE TRIAL COURT that starts an appeal.

When to use it Within 10 days after the judgment becomes final under Rule 81.05.

If you miss it The appeal is ineffective and the appellate court has no jurisdiction. Rule 44.01(b) EXPRESSLY forbids the court from extending the time for filing a notice of appeal under Rule 81.04. Your only remedy is a Rule 81.07 special order.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not applicable.

Pro se note Ten days is brutally short and it runs from FINALITY, not from entry of judgment. With no after-trial motion, that is 30 + 10 = 40 days after entry. File the notice with the TRIAL court clerk, not the appellate court. Use Civil Procedure Form No. 8. Rule 81.04(d): the appellate court docket fee, including all surcharges, is $70 — or file a motion to proceed as a poor person.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200307

Rule 81.07(a): the motion must be filed 'within six months from the date the judgment appealed from became final for purposes of appeal,' and must show that …
the delay was not due to the appellant's culpable negligence.
Rule 81.07(a); Rule 81.07(b)

Motion for special order permitting a late notice of appeal

AppellateVerified

A motion filed in the appellate court asking it to grant a special order allowing you to file a notice of appeal after the 10 days have run.

When to use it When you blew the notice of appeal deadline and the delay was not your fault.

If you miss it After six months there is NO route back. Rule 44.01(b) forbids the court from extending time for action under Rule 81.07 as well.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note This is the only lifeline for a missed appeal, and 'culpable negligence' is a real standard — 'I didn't know the rule' is usually not enough. File it in the appellate court, immediately, with an affidavit explaining the delay.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200310

No day count for the motion. Rule 81.09: the court may fix the amount of the bond and allow the appellant up to 30 days to file it;
when approved and filed, the bond stays execution.
Rule 81.09

Motion for stay of execution / to approve supersedeas bond

AppellateVerified

A motion to stop the judgment creditor from collecting while the appeal is pending, normally by posting a supersedeas bond.

When to use it As soon as you appeal a money judgment you cannot pay.

If you miss it An appeal by itself does NOT stay execution in most cases — the creditor can garnish and levy while you appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note Certain appellants get an automatic stay without bond under Rule 81.09 — executors, administrators, personal representatives, conservators, guardians, curators, and municipalities. Everyone else needs a bond conditioned to satisfy the judgment in full together with costs, interest, and damages for delay. Ask the court to set the amount and to give you the full 30 days.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200312

Rule 81.12: 'Within ten days after the notice of appeal is filed, appellant shall order the transcript';
a written request that unavailable documents be entered must also be made within 10 days after the notice of appeal is filed; a non-system legal file is due within 30 days after the notice of appeal is filed; the transcript certificate is due within 10 days after payment of the charges. Rule 81.19: the record on appeal shall be filed with the appellate court (a) if it consists only of a legal file, within 30 days from the date of filing of the notice of appeal in the trial court; (b) in all other cases, within 90 days from the date of filing of the notice of appeal in the trial court. If more than one appeal is taken from the same judgment to the same appellate court, the time for filing dates from the filing of the last notice of appeal.
Rule 81.12; Rule 81.19

Record on appeal — ordering the transcript and filing the record

AppellateVerified

The appellant's obligation to assemble and file the legal file and transcript the appellate court will decide the case on.

When to use it Immediately after filing the notice of appeal.

If you miss it Rule 84.08(a) — the clerk sets the case on a dismissal docket and, if the default is not cured, the appeal is dismissed.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note You must PAY the court reporter for the transcript; that is often the largest single cost of an appeal. Order it within 10 days and start arranging payment at once. Rule 81.12 was amended March 7, 2023 (eff. Jan. 1, 2024) and August 15, 2023 (eff. July 1, 2024) — get the current version, as the electronic-record provisions changed.
