Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=200315

Rule 84.05(a): appellant's brief within 60 days after the date on which the record on appeal is filed;
respondent's brief within 30 days after the filing of appellant's brief; reply brief within 15 days after the filing of respondent's brief. Cross appeals: appellant's brief 60 days after record; respondent/cross-appellant's brief 30 days after appellant's brief; appellant's response and reply within 30 days after respondent/cross-appellant's brief; cross-appellant's reply within 15 days thereafter.
Rule 84.05(a)-(b); extensions Rule 84.05 ('the court may shorten or lengthen the periods prescribed above for filing briefs'); Rule 44.01(b) generally

Briefs — when due, and motions to extend brief time

AppellateVerified

The briefing schedule in the appellate court and the mechanism for enlarging it.

When to use it After the record on appeal is filed.

If you miss it Rule 84.08 dismissal docket, then dismissal of the appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply See deadline field — the whole rule is a response/reply schedule.

Pro se note Rule 84.05 does not give you any automatic number of extension days — every extension is by motion and by court order. Ask early and in writing; do not simply file late. Rule 84.05 was last amended March 7, 2023, effective January 1, 2024.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199781

UNVERIFIED — neither Rule 84.04 nor Rule 84.06 sets a deadline for a motion to strike a brief.
Practice is to file promptly after the offending brief and before or with your responsive brief.
Rule 84.04(c) (statement of facts), 84.04(d) (points relied on), 84.04(i); Rule 84.06(b) word limits; Rule 84.06(c) certificate of compliance; Rule 84.08 (involuntary dismissal)

Motion to strike a brief for Rule 84.04 noncompliance

AppellateUnverified

A motion asking the appellate court to strike an opponent's brief, or dismiss the appeal, because the brief violates the briefing rules — most often the points relied on.

When to use it When the other side's brief does not comply with Rule 84.04 or exceeds the Rule 84.06 word limits.

If you miss it Nothing forfeited, but the court can and often does act on 84.04 defects on its own.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not set by rule.

Pro se note This rule cuts both ways and it is the leading cause of pro se appeals being dismissed. Rule 84.04(d) requires each point in substantially this form: 'The trial court erred in [identify the challenged ruling or action], because [state the legal reasons for the claim of reversible error], in that [explain why the legal reasons, in the context of the case, support the claim of reversible error].' Abstract statements of law do not comply, and immediately following each point you must list cases, not to exceed four. Rule 84.06(b) word limits: appellant's initial brief and all briefs in a cross appeal except the reply brief, 31,000 words; respondent's brief, 27,900 words; appellant's reply brief, 7,750 words — excluding cover, the Rule 84.06(c) certificate, signature block and appendix. Rule 84.06(c) requires a certificate of compliance stating the word count.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199780

Rule 84.08(a) governs the court's own involuntary dismissal process …
the clerk places the case on a dismissal docket and notifies the parties, and 'The date shall not be less than 15 days from the date of the notice.' No deadline is set for a PARTY's motion to dismiss an appeal.
Rule 84.08(a); Rule 84.08(b) (agency-decision appeals)

Motion to dismiss an appeal / involuntary dismissal

AppellateVerified

A request that the appellate court end the appeal, typically for failure to take a required step or because the judgment is not final and appealable.

When to use it When the appellant defaults on the record or briefs, or when there is no appealable judgment.

If you miss it For the appellant: the appeal is dismissed.

Notes & source

Response / reply The default may be cured before the dismissal docket date.

Pro se note If you receive a dismissal-docket notice, you have at least 15 days to CURE the default — do the missing thing (order the transcript, file the record, file the brief) and file a motion showing you cured it. Do not ignore the notice.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199785

Rule 84.17(b): 'A motion under this Rule 84.17 shall be filed within 15 days after the court files its opinion, written order under Rules 84.16(b) …
or 30.25(b), memorandum decision, or order of dismissal.'
Rule 84.17(a)(1) motion for rehearing; (a)(2) motion to modify; (a)(3) motion to publish an opinion; (b) 15-day deadline; (c) responses; (d) repetitive motions

Motion for rehearing (and motions to modify or to publish)

AppellateVerified

Post-disposition motions in the court of appeals or Supreme Court after an opinion, memorandum decision, written order, or order of dismissal.

When to use it When the court overlooked or misinterpreted a material matter of law or fact.

If you miss it You lose rehearing, and because Rule 83.04's clock keys partly off the disposition of Rule 84.17 motions you may narrow your transfer window.

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 84.17(c): 'No suggestions in opposition to a post-disposition motion shall be filed unless requested by the court. Within ten days after the court's request for the suggestions, any party may file suggestions in opposition to the motion.'

Pro se note Rule 84.17(a)(1): 'Reargument of issues determined by the court will be disregarded.' A rehearing motion that just re-argues your brief is wasted. Point to something specific the opinion overlooked. Rule 84.17(d): if a rehearing motion is overruled and the opinion is not materially modified, you cannot file another one.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199794

Rule 83.02: 'Application by a party for such transfer shall be filed within fifteen days of the date on which the opinion …
memorandum decision, written order, or order of dismissal is filed unless for good cause shown the court of appeals extends the time for filing.'
Rule 83.02

Application for transfer to the Supreme Court filed in the court of appeals

AppellateVerified

An application asking the court of appeals itself to transfer the case to the Supreme Court of Missouri.

When to use it When the case involves a question of general interest or importance, or existing law should be reexamined.

If you miss it You go straight to the Rule 83.04 application instead; but note Rule 83.02's window can be extended by the court of appeals for good cause, unlike most appellate deadlines.

Notes & source

Response / reply 'No response to an application for transfer shall be filed unless requested by the court, and an order for transfer shall not be granted in the absence of such a request.'

Pro se note 'Motions for reconsideration of the court's action in refusing an application for transfer shall not be accepted or filed.' One shot only in the court of appeals.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199766

Rule 83.04: 'Application for such transfer shall be filed in this Court within the later of …
(1) 15 days of the date on which the court of appeals denied transfer or overruled any Rule 84.17 post-disposition motion; or (2) 30 days of the date on which the court of appeals files its opinion, memorandum decision, written order, or order of dismissal.'
Rule 83.04; grounds incorporate Rule 83.02 plus 'that the opinion filed is contrary to a previous decision of an appellate court of this state'

Application for transfer filed in the Supreme Court after a court of appeals opinion

AppellateVerified

An application filed directly in the Supreme Court of Missouri asking it to take the case after the court of appeals has decided it.

When to use it After the court of appeals denies transfer or overrules your post-disposition motion.

If you miss it The court of appeals decision stands and your case is over.

Notes & source

Response / reply 'No response to an application for transfer shall be filed unless requested by the Court.'

Pro se note CAUTION: many sources still describe this as a flat '15 days.' Rule 83.04 was amended June 4, 2024, effective January 1, 2025, and is now the LATER of the two periods above — so a fast denial of transfer does not necessarily shorten you below 30 days from the opinion. Compute both and take the later date.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199768

UNVERIFIED — Rule 84.05(f) addresses amicus curiae briefs in the Supreme Court, but I did not read subdivision (f)'s text this session and cannot state its timing.
Rule 84.05(f) — subdivision text NOT read this session

Motion for leave to file an amicus curiae brief

AppellateUnverified

A non-party's motion for permission to file a friend-of-the-court brief.

When to use it When a non-party has an interest in the legal question.

If you miss it Leave is denied and the brief is not considered.

Notes & source

Response / reply Not verified.

Pro se note Read Rule 84.05(f) directly before relying on any timing. Amicus practice is rarely relevant to a self-represented party's own case.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199781

No deadline is fixed by rule for filing the petition.
Rule 84.24 sets internal timing: a respondent has 10 days after the filing of the petition to file suggestions in opposition, and 'The writ shall not issue before the expiration of the time' for opposition. If the court orders briefing, petitioner has 30 days, respondent 20 days, and the reply brief 10 days. The court may shorten or waive these when the purpose of the writ would be defeated by delay.
Rule 84.24 (procedure as to original writs); Rule 94 (mandamus); Rule 97 (prohibition). Rule 94 provides that proceedings in mandamus in the Supreme Court or court of appeals are governed by Rules 84.22 to 84.26 inclusive and Rule 94, and that mandamus proceedings otherwise conform to the rules of civil procedure and existing rules of general law.

Petition for an original remedial writ in an appellate court (mandamus / prohibition)

AppellateVerified

An ORIGINAL PETITION — not a motion in an existing appeal — asking an appellate court to order a lower court or official to act (mandamus) or to stop acting beyond its authority (prohibition).

When to use it When a trial court is doing something you cannot effectively appeal from later — refusing to rule, ordering privileged discovery, acting without jurisdiction.

If you miss it No forfeiture by delay as such, but writ relief is discretionary and extraordinary; sitting on it undermines the urgency that justifies it.

Notes & source

Response / reply Suggestions in opposition within 10 days of the filing of the petition. 'Oral argument will not be granted on petitions for original remedial writs.'

Pro se note This is a PETITION commencing an original proceeding, not a motion in your case — different caption, its own docket fee (or a motion to proceed as a poor person), and proof of service on the respondent judge AND all parties in the underlying action. Rule 84.24 requires the petition and a writ summary 'which shall not exceed one page exclusive of the caption.' Writs are rarely granted; they are not a substitute for an appeal. Rule 84.24 was amended November 19, 2019, effective July 1, 2020.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199801

Rule 81.045: 'Any time after the notice of appeal is filed with the clerk of the trial court …
the trial court, on its own motion, or on the motion of any party, for good cause shown, may shorten the 30-day period provided for in Rule 75.01.'
Rule 81.045 (adopted by order of October 1, 2024, effective July 1, 2025); Rule 75.01; Rule 81.05(a)

Motion to shorten the Rule 75.01 30-day period (expedited appeal)

AppellateVerified

A brand-new mechanism, created effective July 1, 2025, letting a party get the judgment to finality faster than the normal 30 days so the appeal can start sooner.

When to use it When waiting the full 30 days for finality would prejudice you.

If you miss it Nothing forfeited — you simply wait the normal 30 days.

Notes & source

Response / reply If the trial court overrules the motion, the party seeking to shorten the 30-day period may file a similar motion in the appropriate appellate court, 'which shall decide the matter without deference to the trial court's denial.'

Pro se note The motion must be supported by an AFFIDAVIT stating reasons why you would be prejudiced by the 30-day period before the judgment becomes final. Note the carve-out: 'The provisions of this rule do not apply to judgments resolving fewer than all the remaining claims or parties as provided for in Rule 74.01(b).' This rule is new — most form books and older guides do not mention it at all.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=213129

Missouri criminal 70

Charging / first appearance / release 18

No fixed filing deadline. Rule 33.01(a): a defendant charged with a bailable offense 'shall be entitled to be released from custody pending trial or other …
stage of the criminal proceedings.' Detention requires 'clear and convincing evidence that no combination of non-monetary conditions and monetary conditions will secure the safety of the community or other person' (Rule 33.01(d)). Rule 33.01(f) requires the court to enter a WRITTEN order stating the conditions and the findings supporting them.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 33.01 (amended June 30, 2019, eff. Jan. 1, 2020)

Motion for release on recognizance / for least-restrictive conditions of release

Release / bondVerified

A motion asking the court to release you on your own recognizance, or on the least restrictive non-monetary conditions, instead of a cash or surety bond. Rule 33.01(c) makes ROR the default and requires the court to consider non-monetary conditions before monetary ones.

When to use it At or immediately after the initial appearance, or any time you are detained or held on conditions you cannot meet.

If you miss it You stay in custody or keep paying for a bond the rule may not permit. Because 33.01(f) requires written findings, a court that detains without them gives you a reviewable record - but only if you asked.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 2019 amendments (eff. 1/1/2020) rewrote this rule in the defendant's favor: ROR is the presumption, the court must use the 'least restrictive condition or combination of conditions,' and it must consider non-monetary conditions FIRST. Cite 33.01(c) and (f) by subsection and ask for the written order.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199417

No fixed deadline - available at any stage.
Rule 33.06(a) permits modification when the court finds '(1) New, different, or additional requirements for release are necessary; or (2) The conditions of release which have been set are excessive; or (3) The defendant has failed to comply with or has violated the conditions of release; or (4) The defendant has been convicted of the offense charged.' Requires notice to the parties and a hearing, subject to the victim's right to be informed and heard.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 33.06 (amended Dec. 18, 2018, eff. July 1, 2019; amended June 30, 2019, eff. Jan. 1, 2020)

Motion to modify conditions of release (bond reduction)

Release / bondVerified

The actual, named vehicle in the Missouri rules for reducing a bond or changing release conditions. Rule 33.06(a) lets the court modify conditions on motion of the defendant, the state, or on its own motion, after notice and hearing.

When to use it Any time the conditions set are excessive, you cannot make the bond, or your circumstances have changed.

If you miss it Nothing changes - the court will not reduce a bond on its own. Note the flip side: 33.06(a)(3) and (4) let the STATE use this same rule to increase your conditions, and 33.06(b) lets the court remand you into custody until you comply with the modified conditions.

Notes & source

Pro se note Style it 'Motion to Modify Conditions of Release Pursuant to Rule 33.06,' not 'Motion to Reduce Bond' - it is the same relief but it tracks the rule. Attach the 33.01(e) factors (ties to the community, employment, length of residence, record of appearance) as your argument.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199422

The hearing 'shall occur as soon as practicable but no later than seven days …
excluding weekends and holidays, after the initial appearance, absent good cause shown by the parties or the court.' Rule 33.05 also provides: 'Nothing herein shall prohibit a defendant from making subsequent application for review of the defendant's detention or conditions of release under Rule 33.01.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 33.05 (amended Dec. 18, 2018, eff. July 1, 2019; June 30, 2019, eff. Jan. 1, 2020; Oct. 26, 2021, eff. July 1, 2022)

Request for a release hearing (7-day detention review)

Release / bondVerified

An automatic review hearing for anyone still detained after the initial appearance. The court re-decides detention or conditions under Rule 33.01. You should not have to ask, but you should ask if it is not set.

When to use it Whenever you remain in custody after your initial appearance under Rule 21.10 (misdemeanor) or Rule 22.08 (felony).

If you miss it You can sit in jail well past the seven days with no review. The rule is self-executing but in practice you often have to remind the court in writing.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 7 days EXCLUDE weekends and holidays, so it is really about 9-11 calendar days. If the hearing has not been set, file a one-page 'Request for Release Hearing Pursuant to Rule 33.05' - it is not a motion the court can ignore.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199421

'A defendant so detained shall, upon written request filed after arraignment …
be entitled to a trial which begins within 120 days of the defendant's request or within 120 days of an order granting a change of venue, whichever occurs later. Any request by the defendant to continue the trial beyond the 120 days shall be considered a waiver by the defendant of the right to have the trial conducted within 120 days.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 33.01(d) (amended June 30, 2019, eff. Jan. 1, 2020)

Written request for trial within 120 days (detained defendant)

Release / bondVerified

If the court orders you DETAINED under Rule 33.01(d), you have a rule-based right to a trial within 120 days - but only if you file a written request for it after arraignment.

When to use it Only when the court has entered an order of pretrial detention under Rule 33.01(d). Not available if you are released on conditions.

If you miss it No 120-day clock runs at all. The right is triggered by YOUR written request; it is not automatic.

Notes & source

Pro se note File it AFTER arraignment, not before - the rule says 'filed after arraignment.' And be aware of the trap in the last sentence: if you later ask for a continuance, you waive the 120 days.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199417

No fixed deadline stated in the rule. Rule 33.09 …
'if the defendant or the state allege the court unlawfully detained the defendant[,] failed to detain the defendant, or set inadequate or excessive conditions of release, the defendant or the state may seek remedial writ relief in a higher court pursuant to Rule 84.24.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 33.09 (amended May 25, 2018, eff. Jan. 1, 2019; Dec. 18, 2018, eff. July 1, 2019); procedure under Rule 84.24

Writ application to a higher court challenging detention or excessive conditions

Release / bondVerified

When the trial court unlawfully detains you or sets conditions that are inadequate or excessive, the remedy is a remedial writ in the appellate court, not an appeal.

When to use it After you have asked the trial court under Rule 33.01/33.06 and been refused.

If you miss it You have no direct appeal from a bond ruling - a writ is the only route, so failing to use it means the ruling stands until trial.

Notes & source

Pro se note Rule 84.24 writ practice is technical (suggestions in support, the record, service). Exhaust Rule 33.06 in the trial court first and make a clean written record there, because the writ court reviews what the trial court did.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199425

The preliminary hearing must be held within a reasonable time …
and 'not later than 30 days after the initial appearance if the defendant is in custody, or 60 days if the defendant is not in custody.' 'For good cause, the court may extend the time limits one or more times' (up to an additional 30 days in custody / 60 days out of custody per extension). On a probable-cause finding, the defendant is ordered to appear 'on a day certain as soon as practicable, but not more than 40 days after completion of the preliminary hearing.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 22.09 (adopted Jan. 28, 2002, eff. Jan. 1, 2003; amended Dec. 18, 2018, eff. July 1, 2019; Sept. 1, 2020, eff. Mar. 1, 2021; Oct. 26, 2021, eff. July 1, 2022)

Motion for preliminary hearing / to dismiss for lack of probable cause

DispositiveVerified

In a FELONY case begun by complaint, the preliminary hearing is where the state must show probable cause; if it fails, 'the court shall discharge the defendant.' There is no equivalent proceeding in a misdemeanor case.

When to use it Felony complaints only, before the information is filed. Also the vehicle for asking the court to reconsider detention when the state seeks an extension of the preliminary-hearing deadline.

If you miss it Waiving the preliminary hearing gives up your only pre-information probable-cause test and your best free discovery of the state's case.

Notes & source

Pro se note DOES NOT APPLY TO MISDEMEANORS. Rule 22 is titled 'Procedure Applicable to Felonies Only.' In a misdemeanor case there is no preliminary hearing - your probable-cause challenge has to come as a Rule 24.04(b) motion to dismiss the information instead. Note also Rule 22.09(a) expressly lets you ask for reconsideration of detention or release conditions under Rule 33.05 when the state moves to extend.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199254

'A motion for a bill of particulars may be made before arraignment or within ten days after arraignment or at such later time as the court may permit.' A bill …
of particulars 'may be amended at any time subject to such conditions as justice requires.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 23.04 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Jan. 28, 2002, eff. Jan. 1, 2003)

Motion for bill of particulars

ProceduralVerified

A motion asking the court to make the state spell out the particulars of the charge - what act, when, where - when the information alleges the essential facts but is too vague to let you prepare a defense.

When to use it When the information tracks the statute but does not tell you what you supposedly did (very common in RSMo 455.085 order-of-protection violation counts and in 575.150 resisting counts).

If you miss it You go to trial not knowing which act the state is prosecuting, and you lose the ability to argue variance or double jeopardy later.

Notes & source

Pro se note Ten days after arraignment is short, but the rule has a built-in escape hatch - 'or at such later time as the court may permit' - so a late motion is not automatically dead. File it anyway and ask for leave.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199260

Rule 24.04(b)3: 'The motion shall be made before the plea is entered, but the court may permit it to be made within a reasonable time thereafter.' Rule 24.04(b)2 …
defects in the institution of the prosecution or in the indictment/information 'may be raised only by motion before trial. The motion shall include all such defenses and objections then available to the defendant. Failure to present any such defense or objection as herein provided constitutes a waiver thereof, but the court for cause shown may grant relief from the waiver.' EXCEPTION: 'Lack of jurisdiction or the failure of the indictment or information to charge an offense shall be noticed by the court at any time during the pendency of the proceeding.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.04(b) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion to dismiss the indictment or information

DispositiveVerified

The pretrial motion raising defenses and objections capable of determination without a trial of the general issue. Rule 24.04(b) divides these into defects you MUST raise before trial or waive, and two that can be raised at any time.

When to use it Any defect in how the prosecution was instituted or in the charging document; and at any time for failure to charge an offense or lack of jurisdiction.

If you miss it Every curable defect in the charging document is waived - and the state gets to re-file a corrected information rather than lose the case (Rule 24.04(b)5).

Notes & source

Pro se note Two things survive forever and cannot be waived: (1) the court's lack of jurisdiction, and (2) the information's failure to charge an offense. Everything else must be in ONE motion filed before you plead. So put all of your charging-document attacks in a single Rule 24.04(b) motion.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199275

No numeric deadline in the rule; it is a pretrial motion governed by Rule 24.04(b)3 timing ('before the plea is entered …
but the court may permit it to be made within a reasonable time thereafter'). Rule 24.06(b): 'all defendants shall be tried together unless the court orders a defendant to be tried separately,' and a separate trial is ordered only on WRITTEN motion plus a finding of prejudice.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.06 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended June 10, 1980, eff. Jan. 1, 1981; Sept. 30, 1985, eff. July 1, 1986)

Motion to sever defendants (separate trial from a co-defendant)

ProceduralVerified

A written motion for a separate trial where you are charged with a co-defendant and a joint trial would prejudice you - classically where a co-defendant's out-of-court statement implicates you.

When to use it Only when two or more defendants are charged in the same indictment or information.

If you miss it You are tried alongside evidence that would never be admissible against you alone.

Notes & source

Pro se note The motion MUST be in writing - an oral request will not preserve it. Probably not applicable to a single-defendant misdemeanor case.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199277

No numeric deadline in the rule; pretrial motion timing under Rule 24.04(b)3.
Rule 24.07 requires ALL THREE: '(a) A party files a written motion requesting a separate trial of the offense; (b) A party makes a particularized showing of substantial prejudice if the offense is not tried separately; and (c) The court finds the existence of a bias or discrimination against the party that requires a separate trial of the offense.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.07 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Sept. 30, 1985, eff. July 1, 1986)

Motion to sever offenses (separate trial of counts)

ProceduralVerified

A written motion to try charges separately where trying them together would substantially prejudice you - e.g., trying a 455.085 order-of-protection count together with a 565.056 assault count and a 575.150 resisting count in front of one jury.

When to use it When you are charged with more than one offense in the same information and the counts would contaminate each other.

If you miss it The jury hears all counts at once and the weaker count rides on the stronger one.

Notes & source

Pro se note 'Particularized showing of substantial prejudice' is the whole ballgame - a generic assertion that the jury might confuse the counts will lose. Say exactly what evidence comes in on Count II that would be inadmissible in a stand-alone trial of Count I.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199278

'In felony and misdemeanor cases the application must be filed not later than ten days after the initial plea is entered.
If the designation of the trial judge occurs more than ten days after the initial plea is entered, the application shall be filed within ten days of the designation of the trial judge or prior to commencement of any proceeding on the record, whichever is earlier.' Rule 32.07(d): 'Upon the presentation of a timely application for change of judge, the judge promptly shall sustain the application.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 32.07(a)-(b) (adopted Apr. 20, 1981, eff. Jan. 1, 1982; amended Dec. 21, 1993, eff. Jan. 1, 1995; May 30, 1995, eff. Jan. 1, 1996)

Application for change of judge (no cause required)

ProceduralVerified

A one-time peremptory disqualification of the judge. You do not have to allege or prove any reason, and the judge must sustain it if it is timely.

When to use it Any criminal proceeding, felony or misdemeanor, where you want a different judge.

If you miss it You lose the free, no-questions-asked change of judge forever, and are left with the far harder for-cause route.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the single most time-sensitive motion in a Missouri criminal case: TEN DAYS AFTER THE INITIAL PLEA. It need not be verified and it need not state any reason - 'The applicant need not allege or prove any reason for such change.' Watch the second sentence: if the trial judge is designated later, the clock restarts but ends the moment any on-the-record proceeding begins, whichever is earlier.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199407

Rule 32.09(a): 'Neither the state nor any defendant shall be allowed more than one change of judge in any criminal proceeding except that the exercise of an …
application for change of judge prior to the preliminary examination shall not prohibit a party from filing another application for change of judge if the defendant is held to answer for the charge.' (b): 'No defendant shall be allowed more than one change of venue under Rules 32.01 through 32.09.' (c): 'nothing contained in Rules 32.01 through 32.09, inclusive, shall prohibit a judge from ordering a change of venue or change of judge when fundamental fairness so requires or pursuant to Rule 32.10.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 32.09 (adopted Apr. 20, 1981, eff. Jan. 1, 1982; amended May 30, 1995, eff. Jan. 1, 1996)

Only-one-change limit and the fundamental-fairness exception

ProceduralVerified

The rule limiting each party to one change of judge and one change of venue, with an exception preserving the court's power to act when fundamental fairness requires it.

When to use it Read this before spending your peremptory change; and cite it if you need a second change.

If you miss it You burn your one change on the wrong judge and cannot get another as of right.

Notes & source

Pro se note The felony carve-out in (a) - a change taken before the preliminary examination does not use up your change once you are bound over - does NOT help in a misdemeanor case, because misdemeanors have no preliminary examination. In a misdemeanor you get exactly one.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199409

No deadline stated - a disqualifying interest can be raised whenever it appears.
Rule 32.10: 'If the judge is related to any defendant or has an interest in or has been counsel in the criminal proceedings or disqualifies for any other reason, the judge promptly shall transfer the case to the presiding judge of the circuit for reassignment in accordance with the procedures of Rule 32.07(d).'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 32.10 (adopted Apr. 20, 1981, eff. Jan. 1, 1982; amended May 30, 1995, eff. Jan. 1, 1996); see also Rule 2 (Code of Judicial Conduct), Canon 2.11

Judge's disqualification without application (for-cause / recusal)

ProceduralVerified

The provision requiring a judge to step aside on his or her own when related to a defendant, financially interested, previously counsel, or otherwise disqualified. It is the hook for a motion asking a judge to recuse.

When to use it When the judge has an actual conflict and you have already used (or missed) your Rule 32.07 peremptory change.

If you miss it A biased judge decides your case and you have only plain-error review of the point.

Notes & source

Pro se note Rule 32.10 is written as a duty on the judge, not as a motion for you - so caption yours 'Motion for Disqualification of Judge' and cite Rule 32.10 together with Rule 2-2.11 of the Code of Judicial Conduct, which lists the disqualification grounds in detail.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199410

'A change of venue shall be ordered in any criminal proceeding triable by a jury pending in a county having seventy-five thousand or fewer inhabitants upon the …
filing of a written application therefor by the defendant.' 'In felony and misdemeanor cases the application must be filed not later than ten days after the initial plea is entered.' The defendant 'need not allege or prove any reason'; the application need not be verified.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 32.03 (adopted Apr. 20, 1981, eff. Jan. 1, 1982; amended June 16, 1989; Dec. 21, 1993; May 30, 1995; June 20, 2013, eff. Jan. 1, 2014)

Application for change of venue as a matter of right (small counties)

ProceduralVerified

In a county of 75,000 or fewer inhabitants, a jury-triable criminal case gets a change of venue on request, with no reason required. Marion County is well under 75,000, so this is available.

When to use it Any criminal proceeding triable by a jury in a qualifying county - including a misdemeanor, so long as it is jury-triable.

If you miss it You lose the automatic change and are left with Rule 32.04, which requires proving actual community prejudice.

Notes & source

Pro se note Same TEN DAYS AFTER THE INITIAL PLEA clock as the change of judge - file both together if you want both. Note Rule 32.03(c): instead of transferring the case, the court may just bring in a jury from another county, and Rule 32.03(d) lets everything except the jury trial itself stay in the original county.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199403

'In felony and misdemeanor cases the application must be filed not later than ten days after the initial plea is entered.' The application 'shall set forth the …
reason or reasons for the change of venue. It need not be verified but shall be signed by the defendant or the defendant's attorney.' 'The state, within ten days after the filing of the application for a change of venue, may file a denial'; if a denial is filed the court hears evidence. The court may enlarge the time for the state's denial under Rule 20.01(b).
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 32.04 (adopted Apr. 20, 1981, eff. Jan. 1, 1982; amended June 16, 1989; Dec. 21, 1993; May 30, 1995)

Application for change of venue for cause (community prejudice)

ProceduralVerified

A change of venue on the ground that the inhabitants of the county are prejudiced against you or that the state has undue influence over them. Requires stating reasons; the state may contest.

When to use it Large counties, or a small county where you missed the Rule 32.03 window and can prove actual prejudice.

If you miss it The case is tried in a county where publicity or the victim's local standing has poisoned the jury pool.

Notes & source

Pro se note If the state files no denial within ten days, or the truth of your grounds is within the court's own knowledge, 'a change of venue shall be ordered' - the rule makes it mandatory. So a well-pleaded, uncontested application wins by default.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199404

No filing deadline. RSMo 545.780.1: 'If defendant announces that he is ready for trial and files a request for a speedy trial …
then the court shall set the case for trial as soon as reasonably possible thereafter.' 545.780.2: 'The provisions of this section shall be enforceable by mandamus. Neither the failure to comply with this section nor the state's failure to prosecute shall be grounds for the dismissal of the indictment or information unless the court also finds that the defendant has been denied his constitutional right to a speedy trial.'
RSMo 545.780 (effective Aug. 28, 1986)

Request for speedy trial (statutory)

ProceduralVerified

A statutory announcement that you are ready and a request that the case be set. It is much weaker than its name suggests: it does not create a dismissal remedy.

When to use it Any time you want to force the case onto the docket and start building a constitutional speedy-trial record.

If you miss it Delay accrues without you ever having asserted the right - and assertion of the right is one of the four Barker v. Wingo factors a court weighs on a constitutional claim.

Notes & source

Pro se note Do not expect a dismissal from this statute; there is NO 180-day rule and no automatic sanction. Its real value is (a) mandamus to force a setting and (b) creating the dated written assertion you will need for a constitutional speedy-trial motion later. File it and keep the file-stamped copy.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=545.780

RSMo 217.450.1: request must be in writing to the court and the prosecuting attorney, stating the place of imprisonment and identifying the untried charge.
RSMo 217.460: 'Within one hundred eighty days after the receipt of the request and certificate ... by the court and the prosecuting attorney or within such additional necessary or reasonable time as the court may grant, for good cause shown in open court, the offender or his counsel being present, the indictment, information or complaint shall be brought to trial.' Failure means 'no court of this state shall have jurisdiction ... and the court shall issue an order dismissing the same with prejudice' - but only 'if the court finds that the offender's constitutional right to a speedy trial has been denied.'
RSMo 217.450, 217.460 (effective Aug. 28, 2009); see also RSMo 217.490 (Agreement on Detainers, for out-of-state charges)

Request for final disposition of detainer (180-day rule, UMDDL)

DispositiveVerified

The Uniform Mandatory Disposition of Detainers Law: a person already confined in a Missouri correctional facility can force pending Missouri charges to trial within 180 days, on pain of dismissal with prejudice.

When to use it ONLY if you are serving a sentence in a Department of Corrections facility and a detainer has been lodged on the untried charge. It does not apply to someone at liberty, on bond, or in county jail pretrial.

If you miss it No 180-day clock runs; the detainer sits and the charge stays open indefinitely.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the statute people confuse with 545.780 - it is the one with real teeth, but it is only for prisoners with detainers. Read RSMo 217.460 closely: the 2009 version conditions dismissal on ALSO finding a constitutional speedy-trial violation, so it is not the pure automatic dismissal it is sometimes described as.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=217.460

No rule-based deadline; raise it before trial as a Rule 24.04(b)1 motion ('Any defense or objection which is capable of determination without trial of the …
general issue may be raised before trial by motion'). RSMo 545.780.2 expressly preserves it: dismissal is available where 'the court also finds that the defendant has been denied his constitutional right to a speedy trial.'
U.S. Const. amend. VI; Mo. Const. art. I, sec. 18(a); RSMo 545.780.2; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.04(b)1

Motion to dismiss for violation of the constitutional right to a speedy trial

DispositiveVerified

A constitutional motion (6th Amendment; Mo. Const. art. I, sec. 18(a)) arguing that the delay itself requires dismissal. This - not RSMo 545.780 - is where a dismissal remedy actually lives.

When to use it After substantial delay, especially delay caused by the state, where you asserted the right and can show prejudice.

If you miss it Delay that could have ended the case is instead absorbed into the trial record.

Notes & source

Pro se note The four Barker v. Wingo factors (length of delay, reason for delay, assertion of the right, prejudice) are federal case law, not verified rule text - build the record for each. Your earlier-filed 545.780 request is the evidence for factor three.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=545.780

Discovery and pretrial 17

TWO different clocks. (1) After a felony complaint but before an information/indictment …
'Requests or motions for discovery of material and information as provided in Rule 25.03(a) may be made any time after defendant's initial appearance in court,' and the state must respond within FOURTEEN days of receiving the request. (2) After indictment or the filing of an information: the request must be made 'no later than twenty days after arraignment,' and the state has FOURTEEN days to respond. 'The court may enlarge or shorten the times specified in this rule.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.02 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.03

Written request for disclosure by the state (Rule 25.03 discovery request)

DiscoveryVerified

The core criminal discovery device in Missouri. It is a REQUEST filed with the court and served on the prosecutor - no court order or showing of good cause is needed. It triggers the state's duty to hand over reports, statements, witness lists, expert reports, criminal histories, and surveillance.

When to use it In every case, as early as the rules allow. This is the first thing to file.

If you miss it You lose the automatic, no-order-required route to the state's file and are pushed into Rule 25.04, which requires a motion and a judicial finding that your request is reasonable.

Notes & source

Pro se note TWENTY DAYS AFTER ARRAIGNMENT is the number to calendar. The rule is worded as a 'request,' and Rule 25.03(c) tells you exactly how: 'by filing the request in the court where the case is pending and serving a copy of the request upon counsel for the state.' Note Rule 25.03(d)-(f): the state may give you a redacted 'Defendant's Copy' while giving unredacted material to counsel only - as a pro se defendant this cuts against you, and you may need to show 'good cause' under 25.03(f) to get the redacted information.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199286

No numeric deadline in the rule. Rule 25.04(a) …
'The defense may make a written motion in the court having jurisdiction to try said case requesting the state to disclose material and information not covered by Rule 25.03. Such motion shall specify the material or information sought to be disclosed. If the court finds the request to be reasonable, the court shall order the state to disclose to defendant that material and information requested which is found by the court to be relevant and material to defendant's case.' The court sets the time and manner of disclosure under 25.04(b).
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.04 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018)

Motion for disclosure by court order (material not covered by Rule 25.03)

DiscoveryVerified

A written motion for material outside the automatic Rule 25.03 list. Unlike the 25.03 request, this one requires a motion, specificity, and a judicial finding.

When to use it When you need something the automatic list does not cover - dispatch logs, body-cam beyond the incident reports, personnel or Brady-list material on an officer.

If you miss it You never get the item, and on appeal there is no record of what you asked for.

Notes & source

Pro se note 'Such motion shall specify' - a general request for 'all evidence' will be denied. Name the document, the date, the agency. Rule 25.04(c) also reaches material held by OTHER government personnel: the state must use diligence and good faith to get it, and the court 'shall issue suitable subpoenas or orders' on request.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199288

No deadline - it is a continuing, request-free duty.
Rule 25.03(g): 'The state shall, without written request, disclose to defendant any material or information that tends to negate the guilt of defendant for the charged offense, mitigate the degree of the offense charged, reduce the punishment of the offense charged, and any additional material or information that would be required to be disclosed to comply with Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83 (1963), Giglio v. United States, 405 U.S. 150 (1972), and their progeny.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.03(g) (amended Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018; June 21, 2018; Oct. 26, 2021, eff. July 1, 2022)

Demand for Brady / Giglio material

DiscoveryVerified

The exculpatory-evidence duty, now written directly into the discovery rule. It is one of the few disclosures the state owes you WITHOUT any written request.

When to use it Always. Also worth restating in writing so that a later violation is on the record.

If you miss it Nothing is technically waived - the duty is automatic - but without a written demand you will have a harder time proving the state knew what you wanted.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the 2017/2018 amendments put Brady and Giglio by name into the rule, so you can cite a Missouri RULE rather than only federal case law. Rule 25.03(h) extends the duty to material held by other government personnel (the sheriff's office, the crime lab) with a court-subpoena backstop.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199287

No deadline; it is continuing. Rule 25.08 …
'If after complying with a request for disclosure or order of court, a party discovers information or material that the party would have been required to disclose under the request or order, the party shall furnish this additional information or material to opposing counsel as soon as practicable. If the additions are discovered during trial, the court also shall be notified.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.08 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018)

Invoking the state's continuing duty to disclose

DiscoveryVerified

The rule requiring a party who later finds discoverable material to turn it over as soon as practicable - and to tell the court if it surfaces mid-trial.

When to use it When the state produces something late, or you suspect it is sitting on newly acquired material.

If you miss it Late disclosure goes unchallenged and you lose the sanctions argument under Rule 25.18.

Notes & source

Pro se note This duty runs both ways - if you made a Rule 25.05 disclosure to the state, you owe the same continuing duty.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199292

No numeric deadline - available 'at any time during the course of the proceeding.' Rule 25.18(a) …
'If at any time during the course of the proceeding it is brought to the attention of the court that a party has failed to comply with an applicable discovery rule or an order issued regarding discovery, the court may order the party to make disclosure of material and information not previously disclosed, grant a continuance, exclude such evidence, or enter an order it determines just under the circumstances.' 25.18(b): 'Willful violation by counsel or defendant of an applicable discovery rule or an order regarding discovery may subject counsel or defendant to appropriate sanctions by the court.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.18 (adopted Dec. 23, 2003, eff. July 1, 2004; amended Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018)

Motion to compel disclosure and for sanctions

DiscoveryVerified

The enforcement motion when the state ignores a discovery request or a discovery order. The menu of remedies runs from an order compelling disclosure up to exclusion of the evidence.

When to use it As soon as the fourteen-day response window under Rule 25.02 passes without full compliance.

If you miss it Undisclosed evidence comes in at trial and the appellate court sees no objection and no request for a remedy.

Notes & source

Pro se note Ask for a SPECIFIC remedy, in order of preference (exclusion, then continuance, then an order compelling). A motion that just complains without naming the relief usually produces only an order to produce - which rewards the late disclosure. Note 25.18(b) also exposes YOU to sanctions for willful violation, so comply with any Rule 25.05 request the state makes.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199302

No filing deadline stated; the right attaches once the charging document is filed.
Rule 25.12(a): 'A defendant in any criminal case after an indictment or the filing of an information may obtain the deposition of any person on oral examination or written questions.' Rule 25.12(c): the defendant generally may NOT be physically present at a discovery deposition 'unless both parties agree or the court approves for good cause,' on motion, weighing whether presence aids discovery, the effect on the witness, and whether screening/recording alternatives exist. Rule 25.12(d): for experts, 'Unless manifest injustice would result, the court shall require that the party seeking discovery pay the expert a reasonable hourly fee for the time the expert is deposed.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.12 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Dec. 23, 2003, eff. July 1, 2004; Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018)

Notice of deposition of a state witness

DiscoveryVerified

Missouri is unusual: a criminal defendant may take discovery depositions of witnesses. Under the current text this is not limited to felonies.

When to use it To lock down a complaining witness's account before trial - especially valuable in an order-of-protection violation case where credibility is everything.

If you miss it You meet the state's key witness for the first time on the stand.

Notes & source

Pro se note Two traps for a pro se defendant. (1) The current rule says 'in any criminal case' - it is NOT limited to felonies, contrary to older summaries. (2) Rule 25.12(c) presumes you will NOT be personally present at the deposition; if you are pro se, that creates an obvious problem, so file a Rule 25.12(c) motion for your presence and address the three listed factors. Also budget for the court reporter - depositions are not free.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199296

Rule 24.05: 'Requests that evidence be suppressed shall be raised by motion before trial;
however, the court may in its discretion entertain a motion to suppress evidence at any time during trial.' RSMo 542.296.3: the motion 'shall be made before the commencement of the trial unless the movant was unaware of the grounds or had no opportunity to do so before trial,' in which case the court may entertain it during trial in its discretion. RSMo 542.296.6: 'The burden of going forward with the evidence and the risk of nonpersuasion shall be upon the state to show by a preponderance of the evidence' that the motion should be denied.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.05 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980); RSMo 542.296 (effective Aug. 28, 1974)

Motion to suppress evidence, statements, or identification

Evidentiary / trialVerified

The motion to keep out evidence obtained in violation of the Fourth, Fifth, or Fourteenth Amendments or article I, section 15 of the Missouri Constitution. Rule 24.05 sets the timing; RSMo 542.296 supplies the statutory grounds and, critically, puts the burden on the state.

When to use it Warrantless searches, stops without reasonable suspicion, statements taken without or after Miranda, suggestive show-ups.

If you miss it The evidence comes in unchallenged; and if you never moved to suppress, an appellate court will review at most for plain error.

Notes & source

Pro se note Cite BOTH Rule 24.05 and RSMo 542.296 - the statute is what shifts the burden of proof onto the state, which is the most valuable thing in the motion. Under 542.296.5 the grounds include a warrantless seizure without lawful authority, a defective or unsupported warrant, seizure of property not described in the warrant, illegal execution, and any violation of art. I, sec. 15 or the Fourth and Fourteenth Amendments. Also: a suppression ruling alone does not preserve the issue - you must object again when the evidence is offered at trial, and raise it in the Rule 29.11 new-trial motion.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199276

NO deadline and no Missouri criminal rule governs it - it is a creature of practice, taken up under the court's general control of trial.
Practically, it is filed with the other pretrial motions and heard before jury selection. Rule 20.01(d) supplies the service rule for any written motion: it 'shall be served not later than five days before the time specified for the hearing, unless a different period is fixed by law or court rule or by order of the court.'
No specific rule; timing/service under Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(d); substantive grounds from the rules of evidence

Motion in limine

Evidentiary / trialVerified

A pretrial request for an advance ruling excluding specified evidence or barring a line of argument - prior bad acts, the underlying allegations behind the order of protection, prior convictions.

When to use it Before trial or before opening statements, for evidence whose mere mention would be prejudicial.

If you miss it Prejudicial material is mentioned in front of the jury before you can object, and the bell cannot be unrung.

Notes & source

Pro se note CRITICAL PRESERVATION POINT: a ruling on a motion in limine is interlocutory and preserves NOTHING by itself. You must object again when the evidence is actually offered at trial, and then include the point in your Rule 29.11 motion for new trial. Many pro se appeals die exactly here.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199229

No numeric deadline. Rule 24.09: 'A motion for continuance shall set forth in writing the facts upon which the motion is based.
The motion may be oral if: (a) The adverse party consents, or (b) The court finds good cause.' Where the ground is an absent witness, Rule 24.10 requires the application to show '(a) The facts showing the materiality of the evidence sought to be obtained and due diligence upon the part of the applicant to obtain such witness or testimony'; (b) the name and residence of the witness, or diligence used to learn it; (c) the particular facts the affiant believes the witness will prove and that affiant knows of no other person to prove them; and (d) 'that such witness is not absent by the connivance, consent, or procurement of the applicant, and such application is not made for vexation or delay, but in good faith.' The court may allow amendment of a deficient affidavit.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.09 (adopted Mar. 17, 2003, eff. Jan. 1, 2004); Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.10 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion for continuance

ProceduralVerified

A request to move a setting. Must be in writing and set out the facts, unless the other side consents or the court finds good cause for an oral request.

When to use it When you need time for discovery, an expert, or a witness who cannot be located.

If you miss it You go to trial unprepared, and a denial is nearly unreviewable if your written application did not make the required showing.

Notes & source

Pro se note If a detained defendant has demanded trial within 120 days under Rule 33.01(d), asking for a continuance WAIVES that 120-day right by the rule's own terms. Weigh that before you move. For an absent-witness continuance, hit all four Rule 24.10 items literally - courts deny these for omitting item (d).

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199280

NO deadline and no Missouri criminal rule sets a standard - it rests in the trial court's discretion and is requested orally at the start of the proceeding.
Note that under RSMo 455.085 and Rule 33.01/33.05/33.06, a CRIME VICTIM has a right to be informed of and heard at release proceedings, and Missouri's victims' rights provisions may entitle a victim to remain present.
No specific criminal rule; trial court discretion

Motion to exclude or sequester witnesses

Evidentiary / trialUnverified

A request that witnesses be excluded from the courtroom until they testify, so their testimony is not shaped by what they hear.

When to use it At the start of trial or any evidentiary hearing, including the suppression hearing.

If you miss it Later witnesses hear and conform to earlier testimony.

Notes & source

Pro se note Make the request on the record before the first witness is sworn. Expect that the complaining witness may still be allowed to stay under victims' rights provisions - ask instead that the witness be sequestered until after he or she testifies.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199309

No filing deadline - competency may be raised at any stage.
RSMo 552.020.2: 'the judge shall, upon his or her own motion or upon motion filed by the state or by or on behalf of the accused' order the examination. After the report is filed, RSMo 552.020.7 gives a party 'within ten days after the filing of the report' to request its own independent examination by a qualified professional of its choosing at its own expense.
RSMo 552.020 (effective Aug. 28, 2023, A.L. 2023 S.B. 106)

Motion for mental examination / to determine competency to proceed

ProceduralVerified

A motion suspending the proceedings so a qualified examiner can evaluate whether you have the capacity to understand the proceedings and assist in your defense.

When to use it Whenever there is reasonable cause to believe a defendant lacks that capacity.

If you miss it An incompetent defendant is tried, which is a due process violation - but the ten-day window to obtain your own examiner closes quickly once the state's report lands.

Notes & source

Pro se note Calendar the TEN DAYS from the filing of the examination report if you intend to get a second opinion. Note this is separate from the insanity defense in 552.030 - competency is about NOW, insanity is about THEN.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=552.020

'[W]ithin ten days after a plea of not guilty …
or at such later date as the court may for good cause permit, the accused files a written notice' of the purpose to rely on the defense.
RSMo 552.030.2 (effective Aug. 28, 2023, A.L. 2023 S.B. 106); see also Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.05(a)(4) (disclosure to the state on written request)

Notice of intent to rely on mental disease or defect excluding responsibility (NGRI)

ProceduralVerified

The written notice that must be filed before you can raise the insanity defense. Without it, the defense is unavailable.

When to use it When the defense is that you lacked capacity to appreciate the wrongfulness of your conduct or to conform it to the law at the time of the offense.

If you miss it The insanity defense is not available at trial.

Notes & source

Pro se note TEN DAYS AFTER THE NOT GUILTY PLEA - the same short fuse as the change of judge. The statute does allow a later filing 'for good cause,' but do not rely on it. Separately, Rule 25.05(a)(4) requires you to disclose reliance on mental disease or defect to the state, in the form of a written statement, if the state requests it.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=552.030

No independent deadline; triggered by the state's written request under Rule 25.05.
Rule 25.05(a)(5): 'If defendant intends to rely on the defense of alibi and the state in its request specifies the place, date, and time of the crime charged, disclosure shall be in the form of a written statement by counsel for defendant, announcing defendant's intent and giving specific information as to the place at which defendant claims to have been at the time of the alleged offense, and as particularly as is known, the names and addresses of the witnesses by whom defendant proposes to establish the alibi.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 25.05(a)(5) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Dec. 19, 2017, eff. July 1, 2018)

Alibi disclosure (notice of alibi)

DiscoveryVerified

Missouri has no free-standing alibi-notice rule; the obligation arises only if the STATE requests it and specifies the place, date, and time of the offense.

When to use it When your defense is that you were somewhere else - relevant to an order-of-protection violation count that turns on your presence at a location.

If you miss it Your alibi witnesses can be excluded as a Rule 25.18 discovery sanction.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the condition precedent: the state must specify the place, date, and time in ITS request. If the state's request is vague, your disclosure obligation is not triggered - but do not play games with this; the safer course is to disclose and preserve the objection. There is NO comparable notice requirement in the rules for self-defense or justification.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199289

No numeric deadline; must be made before trial with the court's approval.
Rule 27.01(a): 'All issues of fact in any criminal case shall be tried by a jury ... unless trial by jury be waived as provided in this Rule.' Rule 27.01(b): the defendant may, with the court's approval, waive a jury; 'In felony cases, such waiver by the defendant shall be entered on the record and made in open court in person or by interactive video technology upon the defendant's waiver of his or her right to be physically present and with the court's approval.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 27.01 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Oct. 26, 2021, eff. July 1, 2022)

Waiver of jury trial / election of court-tried case

ProceduralVerified

The formal waiver of your right to a jury and submission of the case to the judge.

When to use it When the facts are technical or the charge is one a jury would react to emotionally - and only after very careful thought.

If you miss it You cannot un-waive a jury once trial begins.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the on-the-record, in-open-court formality in 27.01(b) is stated for FELONY cases; for misdemeanors the rule is less formal, but insist on an on-the-record waiver anyway so there is no dispute later. Also note the knock-on effect: in a court-tried case Rule 29.11(e) says a motion for new trial is NOT necessary to preserve issues for appellate review.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199310

RSMo 542.301 sets outer limits: a claim for stolen or lawfully acquired property must be made within ONE YEAR of the seizure;
a further SIX MONTHS after that year to file disposition motions before the property is deemed abandoned; property unclaimed for THREE YEARS after seizure is deemed abandoned and passes to the state treasurer. On forfeiture, the state must prove the property is subject to forfeiture by CLEAR AND CONVINCING evidence.
RSMo 542.301 (effective Aug. 28, 2026, A.L. 2026 H.B. 2273 et al.)

Motion for return of seized property

ProceduralVerified

A written motion by the person entitled to possession asking the court to order property returned - filed in the court where a suppression motion has been or could be filed.

When to use it When a phone, firearm, or other property was seized during the arrest and is not needed as evidence.

If you miss it Your property is deemed abandoned and forfeited to the state by operation of the statute.

Notes & source

Pro se note The one-year clock runs from the SEIZURE, not from the end of your case - so do not wait for the criminal case to conclude. Note this section was amended effective August 28, 2026; the version summarized here is the one currently in force.

Source read revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=542.301

No numeric deadline. Rule 31.02(a): 'In all criminal cases the defendant shall have the right to appear and defend in person and by counsel.' If the charge …
could result in confinement and the defendant appears without counsel, the court must advise of the right to counsel and of the court's willingness to appoint if the defendant cannot afford one; appointment is mandatory on proof of indigency. A knowing waiver after full advice excuses appointment, but 'the court may override a waiver if circumstances suggest this would cause injustice.' 'Appointed counsel shall be allowed a reasonable time in which to prepare the defense.' Rule 31.02(b): where an unrepresented defendant waives counsel at a FELONY arraignment, the court reporter must prepare and file a transcript. Rule 31.02(c): 'If an indigent defendant convicted of an offense and sentenced to confinement desires to appeal, the trial court shall appoint counsel to represent him.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 31.02 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980); Faretta v. California, 422 U.S. 806 (1975)

Request to proceed pro se / for appointment of counsel / for standby counsel

ProceduralVerified

The rule governing the right to counsel, the court's duty to advise you of it, and the conditions on waiving it. It is also the rule the court will use to test whether your self-representation is knowing and voluntary.

When to use it At the first appearance and any time your representation status changes.

If you miss it If your waiver of counsel was not knowing and voluntary on the record, the conviction is vulnerable - but that cuts both ways, and a court may simply refuse to let you proceed pro se.

Notes & source

Pro se note Rule 31.02 contains NO right to standby or hybrid counsel - that is discretionary with the court. Ask for it expressly and get the ruling on the record. Note 31.02(c) is genuinely useful to you: if you are convicted and SENTENCED TO CONFINEMENT and are indigent, the trial court must appoint appellate counsel, even though you tried the case pro se.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199398

'A motion to withdraw a plea of guilty may be made only before sentence is imposed or when imposition of sentence is suspended;
but to correct manifest injustice the court after sentence may set aside the judgment of conviction and permit the defendant to withdraw his or her plea.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.07(d) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Feb. 11, 1987; May 19, 2010; Oct. 26, 2021, eff. July 1, 2022)

Motion to withdraw a plea of guilty (before sentencing)

ProceduralVerified

A motion to take back a guilty plea. The standard is far more forgiving before sentence is imposed than after.

When to use it Any time after you plead but before sentence is pronounced, or while imposition of sentence is suspended.

If you miss it After sentencing you must meet the much harder 'manifest injustice' standard.

Notes & source

Pro se note There is a separate, easier route at the plea itself: under Rule 24.02(d)(4), if the court REJECTS a plea agreement made under Rule 24.02(d)(1)(A), (C) or (D), the court must give you the chance to withdraw the plea right then. But under 24.02(d)(2), if the agreement is only a non-binding RECOMMENDATION under (d)(1)(B), the court must warn you that the plea CANNOT be withdrawn if the court does not follow it. Know which kind of agreement you have before you plead.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199347

Trial 4

Made at trial when the state's evidence closes.
Rule 27.07(a): 'Motions for directed verdict are abolished and motions for judgment of acquittal shall be used in their place.' The court 'shall order the entry of judgment of acquittal of one or more offenses charged in the indictment or information after the evidence on either side is closed if the evidence is insufficient to sustain a conviction of such offense or offenses.' 'If a defendant's motion for judgment of acquittal at the close of the evidence offered by the state is not granted, the defendant may offer evidence without having reserved the right.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 27.07(a) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion for judgment of acquittal at the close of the state's evidence

DispositiveVerified

The motion arguing the state's evidence, standing alone, is legally insufficient to sustain a conviction. It replaces the old motion for directed verdict.

When to use it The moment the state rests.

If you miss it In a jury case the sufficiency challenge is weakened; make it at both stages to be safe.

Notes & source

Pro se note Make it count by count and by element - 'the state failed to prove that a valid full order of protection had been served on the defendant' beats 'the state failed to prove its case.' Note the last sentence of 27.07(a): putting on your own evidence after a denied motion does not forfeit anything.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199316

Made at the close of all the evidence. Rule 27.07(b) …
'If a motion for judgment of acquittal is made at the close of all the evidence, the court may reserve decision on the motion, submit the case to the jury and decide the motion either before the jury returns a verdict or after it returns a verdict of guilty or is discharged without having returned a verdict.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 27.07(a)-(b) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion for judgment of acquittal at the close of all the evidence

DispositiveVerified

The renewed sufficiency motion after both sides rest. The court may reserve ruling and submit the case to the jury anyway.

When to use it Immediately after all evidence closes and before submission.

If you miss it Sufficiency is one of the few issues Rule 29.11(d) says need not be in the new-trial motion, but do not rely on that - renew the motion and brief it anyway.

Notes & source

Pro se note Rule 29.11(d) expressly exempts 'sufficiency of conviction evidence' (along with jurisdiction over the offense and whether the indictment/information states an offense) from the requirement of inclusion in a motion for new trial in jury-tried cases. That is a real safety net - but it is narrow, so do not treat it as covering evidentiary rulings.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199316

NO deadline in the rules and no Missouri criminal rule governs it - it must be requested IMMEDIATELY when the event occurs, or it is waived.
It rests entirely in the trial court's discretion and is described in Missouri case law as a drastic remedy.
No specific rule; trial court discretion. Related: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.12(a) (harmless error), 29.12(b) (plain error)

Motion for mistrial

Evidentiary / trialUnverified

A request to abort the trial because something has occurred that no instruction can cure.

When to use it After an incurable disclosure of inadmissible evidence, improper argument, or juror misconduct.

If you miss it Waiting until a break, or asking only for an instruction to disregard, waives the mistrial request.

Notes & source

Pro se note Follow the ladder in order and on the record: object, ask that the jury be instructed to disregard, then request a mistrial. If you skip a rung, the appellate court will say you got all the relief you asked for. Then put the point in your Rule 29.11 new-trial motion.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199352

'No party may assign as error the giving or failure to give instructions or verdict forms unless the party objects thereto before the jury retires to consider …
its verdict, stating distinctly the matter objected to and the grounds of the objection. Counsel need not repeat objections already made on the record prior to delivery of the instructions and verdict forms. The objections must also be raised in the motion for new trial in accordance with Rule 29.11.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 28.03 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Aug. 19, 1994, eff. July 1, 1995)

Objections to jury instructions and verdict forms

Evidentiary / trialVerified

The specific, on-the-record objection required to preserve any complaint about instructions. It is a two-step preservation requirement.

When to use it At the instruction conference, before the jury retires.

If you miss it Instructional error is reviewable only for plain error under Rule 29.12(b) / Rule 30.20 - a very hard standard.

Notes & source

Pro se note TWO steps, both mandatory: (1) object before the jury retires, stating distinctly the matter and the GROUNDS; and (2) raise it again in the Rule 29.11 motion for new trial. A generic 'objection' with no stated ground preserves nothing. Missouri uses pattern instructions (MAI-CR); if you propose an instruction, identify it by MAI-CR number or state why no pattern instruction fits.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199339

Post-trial and sentencing 9

'a motion for judgment of acquittal may be made or renewed within fifteen days after the return of the verdict or the jury is discharged.
On application of the defendant made within fifteen days after the return of the verdict or the discharge of the jury and for good cause shown the court may extend the time for filing or renewing the motion for judgment of acquittal for an additional period not to exceed ten days.' 'It shall not be necessary to the making of such a motion that a similar motion has been made prior to the submission of the case to the jury.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 27.07(c) (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion for judgment of acquittal after verdict / after discharge of the jury

Post-trialVerified

The post-verdict sufficiency motion asking the court to set aside a guilty verdict and enter an acquittal outright - a stronger remedy than a new trial.

When to use it After a guilty verdict, or after the jury is discharged without a verdict.

If you miss it You lose the chance at an outright acquittal from the trial judge and are left with new-trial relief only.

Notes & source

Pro se note Same 15 days + 10 structure as the motion for new trial, and the extension application must itself be made WITHIN the original 15 days. File this together with your Rule 29.11 motion for new trial as alternative relief in the same document, captioned to make both requests clear.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199316

Rule 29.11(b): the motion must be filed 'within fifteen days after the return of the verdict.' 'The court may extend this period by up to ten additional days …
if the defendant applies within the initial fifteen-day window and demonstrates good cause.' Rule 29.11(g): 'An unresolved new trial motion is denied for all purposes if not addressed within ninety days of filing, excluding days when the court lacks authority to act.' Rule 29.11(f): affidavits may be filed and shall be served with the motion; the opposing party has ten days to respond, extendable to thirty days total by court order or stipulation. Rule 29.11(c): no judgment may be entered until the new-trial deadline passes.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.11 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980; amended Aug. 1, 1979; Aug. 19, 1994, eff. July 1, 1995; Dec. 18, 2001, eff. July 1, 2002)

Motion for new trial

Post-trialVerified

The central post-trial motion. In a JURY-tried case it is also the document that preserves nearly every claim of error for appeal.

When to use it After a guilty verdict in a jury trial. Optional (but permitted) in a court-tried case.

If you miss it TOTAL WAIVER of nearly every trial error. In a jury case, error not in this motion is reviewable only for plain error under Rule 29.12(b) / 30.20. This is the single most consequential deadline in a Missouri criminal case after conviction.

Notes & source

Pro se note ABSOLUTE OUTER LIMIT 25 DAYS (15 + 10), and Rule 20.01(b) says the court 'may not enlarge the period for filing a motion for new trial or for taking an appeal.' Even the judge cannot save you past 25 days. The 15 days run from the RETURN OF THE VERDICT, not from sentencing. Rule 29.11(d) exempts only three things from the inclusion requirement in jury cases: jurisdiction over the offense, whether the indictment/information states an offense, and sufficiency of the evidence. Everything else - every evidentiary ruling, every instruction, every closing-argument objection - must be written into this motion, with specificity.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199351

Rule 29.11(e): the defendant may file such a motion 'within the timeframe in subsection (b)' (fifteen days after the finding …
extendable by up to ten days on application made within the fifteen), 'though not required. A new trial motion is unnecessary to preserve appellate review issues in non-jury trials,' except that the same three exceptions in 29.11(d) apply if one is filed.
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.11(e) (amended Aug. 19, 1994, eff. July 1, 1995; Dec. 18, 2001, eff. July 1, 2002)

Motion for new trial in a court-tried (bench) case

Post-trialVerified

The optional new-trial motion where the judge, not a jury, was the fact-finder.

When to use it After a guilty finding in a bench trial - including most misdemeanor trials where a jury was waived.

If you miss it Nothing is automatically waived in a bench trial - but you also gain nothing by silence, and a motion is your only chance to have the trial judge fix the error first.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is a genuine advantage of a bench trial for a pro se defendant: you do not forfeit your appellate issues by failing to file a new-trial motion. Do not rely on it blindly - if you DO file one, make it complete, and objections at trial are still required to preserve evidentiary points.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199351

Rule 29.13(a): 'Within thirty days after the entry of the judgment and prior to the filing of the transcript of the record in the appellate court …
the court may of its own initiative or on motion of a defendant arrest or set aside a judgment upon either of the following grounds: (1) that the facts stated in the indictment or information do not constitute an offense; or (2) that the court is without jurisdiction of the offense charged. The court shall specify of record the grounds upon which the order is entered.' Rule 29.13(b): the court may, with the defendant's consent, order a new trial on its own initiative before entry of judgment 'but not later than thirty days after the verdict of the jury is returned.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.13 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion in arrest of judgment / to set aside the judgment

Post-trialVerified

A motion attacking the judgment on the ground that the charging document does not state an offense or that the court lacked jurisdiction over the offense. These are the two defects that never waive.

When to use it After conviction, within the 30-day window, where the information fails to charge a crime.

If you miss it You lose this specific trial-court remedy - though the same two grounds survive on appeal under Rule 30.20 and can be noticed at any time under Rule 24.04(b)2.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the current home of what older sources call the 'motion in arrest of judgment.' Two hard limits: THIRTY DAYS after entry of judgment, AND before the transcript is filed in the appellate court - whichever comes first.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199353

No deadline stated in the rule. Rule 29.05 in full …
'The court shall have power to reduce the punishment within the statutory limits prescribed for the offense if it finds that the punishment is excessive.'
Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.05 (adopted June 13, 1979, eff. Jan. 1, 1980)

Motion to reduce punishment as excessive

SentencingVerified

A short, direct request that the trial court cut the sentence within the statutory range because it is excessive.

When to use it At or immediately after sentencing, or with the post-trial motions.

If you miss it Nothing is waived by the rule's terms, but as a practical matter the trial court loses authority once the judgment becomes final and the appeal is taken.

Notes & source

Pro se note The rule sets no deadline, which is unusual and useful - but do not rely on it after the judgment is final. File it with your Rule 29.11 motion. It only permits reduction WITHIN the statutory range, so identify the range for the class of misdemeanor and where your sentence sits in it.

Source read www.courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=199345

No deadline stated in the statute. RSMo 558.046 …
