When to use it Only after an order to compel already issued and was disobeyed.

If you miss it Continued noncompliance. Note this cuts both ways — a pro se plaintiff who ignores a discovery order can have the case dismissed under 37(b)(2)(A)(v).

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_37

No fixed deadline; typically raised in a motion in limine or in summary-judgment briefing.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 37(c)(1)

Motion to exclude for failure to disclose (Rule 37(c))

DiscoveryVerified

Seeks automatic exclusion of a witness or document that was not disclosed under Rule 26(a) or 26(e).

When to use it At summary judgment or in limine, when the other side relies on something never disclosed.

If you miss it The undisclosed evidence comes in. Conversely, YOUR undisclosed evidence is excluded "unless the failure was substantially justified or is harmless."

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_37

No fixed deadline.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 37(d)(1), 37(d)(3)

Motion for sanctions — failure to appear at deposition or respond to discovery (Rule 37(d))

DiscoveryVerified

Sanctions for a party who fails to appear at its own properly noticed deposition, or fails to serve any answers to interrogatories or any response to a request for production.

When to use it When the failure is total, not merely inadequate.

If you miss it No sanction obtained. Note that for interrogatories and document requests (but not depositions), Rule 37(d)(1)(B) requires a certification of good-faith conferral.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_37

No fixed deadline.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 37(e)(1), 37(e)(2)

Motion for spoliation sanctions — lost ESI (Rule 37(e))

DiscoveryVerified

The exclusive remedy when electronically stored information that should have been preserved is lost because a party failed to take reasonable steps, and it cannot be restored or replaced.

When to use it Highly relevant in an employment case: deleted emails, purged HR systems, wiped laptops, overwritten timekeeping data.

If you miss it Loss of the inference. Note the two tiers: under 37(e)(1), on a finding of prejudice, the court "may order measures no greater than necessary to cure the prejudice." The severe sanctions — adverse-inference instruction, dismissal, default — require a finding under 37(e)(2) "that the party acted with the intent to deprive another party of the information's use in the litigation."

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note Send a written litigation-hold letter to the employer as early as possible. It is the evidence that the duty to preserve attached, which is what makes 37(e) available later.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_37

"The objection must be served before the earlier of the time specified for compliance or 14 days after the subpoena is served" (Rule 45(d)(2)(B)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 45(d)(2)(B)

Written objection to a document subpoena (Rule 45(d)(2)(B))

DiscoveryVerified

A non-party's written objection to producing documents — a lower-cost alternative to a motion to quash. Serving it shifts the burden to the subpoenaing party to move for an order compelling production.

When to use it When you or a non-party (your doctor, a former co-worker) is subpoenaed for records.

If you miss it Objections are waived and compliance is required.

Notes & source

Response / reply After objection, the serving party may move the court for an order compelling production.

Pro se note This is the cheapest and fastest tool against an overbroad records subpoena — a letter, not a motion — but the window is very short.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_45

Must be "timely" — Rule 45(d)(3)(A) says the court "must quash or modify a subpoena that: (i) fails to allow a reasonable time to comply;
(ii) requires a person to comply beyond the geographical limits specified in Rule 45(c); (iii) requires disclosure of privileged or other protected matter, if no exception or waiver applies; or (iv) subjects a person to undue burden." No number of days is stated; file before the compliance date.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 45(d)(3)(A), 45(d)(3)(B), 45(c), 45(g)

Motion to quash or modify a subpoena (Rule 45(d)(3))

DiscoveryVerified

Asks the court to invalidate or narrow a subpoena.

When to use it When a subpoena is oppressive, seeks privileged material, or reaches beyond the geographic limits.

If you miss it Compliance is required and Rule 45(g) allows contempt for failing without adequate excuse to obey.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note Rule 45(c): a deposition or production can be commanded only "within 100 miles of where the person resides, is employed, or regularly transacts business in person." That geographic limit is a mandatory quash ground.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_45

No fixed number of days — governed by the discovery cutoff.
"The order may be made only on motion for good cause and on notice to all parties and the person to be examined" (Rule 35(a)(2)(A)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 35(a)(1), 35(a)(2)(A), 35(a)(2)(B), 35(b)

Motion for a physical or mental examination (Rule 35)

DiscoveryVerified

Asks the court to order a party to submit to an examination by a licensed examiner when that party's "mental or physical condition—including blood group—is in controversy."

When to use it Directly relevant to an ADA or FMLA case: the employer will typically move for a Rule 35 exam once you put your condition at issue.

If you miss it Refusing a validly ordered exam exposes you to Rule 37(b)(2)(A) sanctions.

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C): 14 days / 10 days. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days / 14 days.

Pro se note Rule 35(a)(2)(B) requires the order to "specify the time, place, manner, conditions, and scope of the examination, as well as the person or persons who will perform it" — negotiate those terms. And be careful with Rule 35(b): REQUESTING the examiner's report waives "any privilege it may have—in that action or any other action involving the same controversy—concerning testimony about all examinations."

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_35

No fixed deadline. Leave is required if the parties have not stipulated and: the deposition would result in more than 10 depositions by that side;
"the deponent has already been deposed in the case"; the party seeks it before the Rule 26(d) time; or "the deponent is confined in prison."
Fed. R. Civ. P. 30(a)(2), 30(b)(1), 30(b)(6), 30(d)(1)

Motion for leave to take a deposition (Rule 30(a)(2))

DiscoveryVerified

Court permission required in four situations where a deposition is not available as of right.

When to use it When you have already taken 10 depositions, want to re-depose someone, need discovery before the Rule 26(f) conference, or the deponent is in prison.

If you miss it The deposition is improper and may be quashed, with expenses shifted.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note Rule 30(d)(1) limits a deposition to "1 day of 7 hours" absent stipulation or court order. Rule 30(b)(6) lets you depose the EMPLOYER as an entity on listed topics — often the most valuable deposition in an employment case — and the parties "must confer in good faith about the matters for examination."

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_30

Made "at any time during a deposition" (Rule 30(d)(3)(A)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 30(d)(3)(A), 30(d)(3)(B), 30(d)(3)(C)

Motion to terminate or limit a deposition (Rule 30(d)(3))

DiscoveryVerified

An emergency motion made during a deposition being "conducted in bad faith or in a manner that unreasonably annoys, embarrasses, or oppresses the deponent or party."

When to use it Mid-deposition, when questioning becomes abusive. The deposition is suspended for the time needed to obtain the order.

If you miss it The deposition continues. Rule 30(d)(3)(C) applies Rule 37(a)(5) to expenses.

Notes & source

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_30

Cross-questions: "within 14 days after being served with the notice and direct questions." Redirect questions …
"within 7 days after being served with cross-questions." Recross-questions: "within 7 days after being served with redirect questions" (Rule 31(a)(5)). The court may alter these.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 31(a)(1), 31(a)(2), 31(a)(5), 31(b), 31(c)

Deposition by written questions (Rule 31)

DiscoveryVerified

A low-cost alternative to an oral deposition: questions are served in writing and an officer reads them to the deponent.

When to use it When you cannot afford a court reporter for a full oral deposition or need testimony from a distant records custodian.

If you miss it You lose the chance to ask cross, redirect, or recross questions.

Notes & source

Response / reply 14 / 7 / 7 days as above.

Pro se note Counts against the same 10-deposition limit as Rule 30 depositions.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_31

Pretrial 4

No deadline set by rule — available whenever "actions before the court involve a common question of law or fact."
Fed. R. Civ. P. 42(a)

Motion to consolidate actions (Rule 42(a))

ProceduralVerified

Combines separate actions before the court that involve a common question of law or fact, for hearing, trial, or all purposes.

When to use it If you have parallel suits — e.g., an FMLA case and a later retaliation case — pending in the same district.

If you miss it Duplicative litigation and duplicated cost; no waiver.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_42

No deadline set by rule; practically governed by the pretrial schedule.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 42(b)

Motion for separate trials / severance (Rule 42(b))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to try separate claims or issues separately "for convenience, to avoid prejudice, or to expedite and economize."

When to use it To bifurcate liability from damages, or to separate a prejudicial claim.

If you miss it No waiver, but a request on the eve of trial is likely denied.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note Rule 42(b) expressly requires the court to "preserve any federal right to a jury trial" when ordering separate trials.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_42

"must be made at least 30 days before trial." Objections must be served "within 14 days after they are made" (Rule 26(a)(3)(B)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 26(a)(3)(A), 26(a)(3)(B)

Rule 26(a)(3) pretrial disclosures

DiscoveryVerified

Final identification of trial witnesses, deposition designations, and exhibits.

When to use it In the run-up to trial.

If you miss it An objection to admissibility (other than under FRE 402 or 403) not made within 14 days is waived unless excused for good cause.

Notes & source

Response / reply 14 days to object after the disclosures are made.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_26

No fixed deadline, but the standard is demanding: "A schedule may be modified only for good cause and with the judge's consent" (Rule 16(b)(4)).
Good cause turns on the diligence of the party seeking the change.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 16(b)(4); Fed. R. Civ. P. 16(b)(2), 16(b)(3)

Motion to modify the scheduling order (Rule 16(b)(4))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the judge to move a case-management date — discovery cutoff, amendment deadline, dispositive-motion deadline.

When to use it Any time a scheduling date cannot realistically be met. File BEFORE the date passes.

If you miss it The original date binds. Rule 16(f) authorizes sanctions, including Rule 37(b)(2)(A)(ii)-(vii) sanctions and mandatory fee-shifting under 16(f)(2), for failing to obey a scheduling order.

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B): 7 days for a motion for extension of time; 14 days otherwise. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days.

Pro se note Rule 16(b)(2): the court must issue the scheduling order "within the earlier of 90 days after any defendant has been served with the complaint or 60 days after any defendant has appeared." That order, not the FRCP defaults, runs your case.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_16

Dispositive motions 2

"Unless a different time is set by local rule or the court orders otherwise …
a party may file a motion for summary judgment at any time until 30 days after the close of all discovery" (Rule 56(b)). In practice the scheduling order almost always sets an earlier date that controls.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 56(a), 56(b), 56(c), 56(e)

Motion for summary judgment (Rule 56)

DispositiveVerified

Asks the court to enter judgment without a trial because "there is no genuine dispute as to any material fact and the movant is entitled to judgment as a matter of law." The stage where most employment cases are decided.

When to use it After discovery closes. In an FMLA/ADA case, expect the employer to file one.

If you miss it Failing to respond properly is close to fatal. Rule 56(e): if a party fails to properly address another party's assertion of fact, the court may "consider the fact undisputed for purposes of the motion" and grant summary judgment.

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B): memorandum in opposition within 14 days after service of the motion; L.R. 4.01(C): reply within 10 days after service of the opposition. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: for summary judgment motions specifically, suggestions in opposition within 21 days; reply within 14 days after the opposing suggestions are filed.

Pro se note You cannot beat summary judgment with your complaint. You must cite actual record evidence — declarations under 28 U.S.C. § 1746, deposition pages, documents. And you MUST answer the statement of facts paragraph by paragraph (see the local-rule entries).

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_56

No independent deadline — file with (or in place of) your opposition, within the local-rule response time.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 56(d)

Rule 56(d) request to defer or deny summary judgment for more discovery

ProceduralVerified

A sworn declaration showing "that, for specified reasons, it cannot present facts essential to justify its opposition." The court may defer or deny the motion, allow time for discovery, or issue any other appropriate order.

When to use it When the employer moves for summary judgment before you have obtained key discovery.

If you miss it The court decides the motion on the record you have. Courts routinely refuse to consider a 'we need more discovery' argument that was not presented in a proper Rule 56(d) affidavit or declaration.

Notes & source

Response / reply Same as the underlying summary-judgment response schedule.

Pro se note It must be an affidavit or declaration, and it must identify the SPECIFIC facts you expect to obtain and why you could not get them. A general complaint about discovery will not do.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_56

Emergency relief 2

A TRO may issue without notice only if "specific facts in an affidavit or a verified complaint clearly show that immediate and irreparable injury …
loss, or damage will result to the movant before the adverse party can be heard in opposition" AND "the movant's attorney certifies in writing any efforts made to give notice and the reasons why it should not be required" (Rule 65(b)(1)). DURATION: an order issued without notice expires at a time the court sets, "not to exceed 14 days," unless the court for good cause extends it or the adverse party consents to a longer extension (Rule 65(b)(2)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(b)(1), 65(b)(2), 65(b)(3), 65(b)(4), 65(c), 65(d)

Motion for a temporary restraining order (Rule 65(b))

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

Emergency, very short-term relief to preserve the status quo, obtainable without notice to the other side only in narrow circumstances.

When to use it When irreparable harm will occur before a preliminary-injunction hearing can be held.

If you miss it If the TRO expires without a preliminary-injunction hearing, the relief lapses. Rule 65(b)(3): if the order issues without notice, "the motion for a preliminary injunction must be set for hearing at the earliest possible time."

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 65(b)(4): the adverse party may appear and move to dissolve or modify the order "on 2 days' notice to the party who obtained the order without notice—or on shorter notice set by the court," and the court must hear and decide the motion "as promptly as justice requires."

Pro se note Rule 65(c) generally requires security "in an amount that the court considers proper to pay the costs and damages sustained by any party found to have been wrongfully enjoined or restrained." Address the bond in your motion — ask the court to set a nominal amount given indigency.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_65

No number of days. "The court may issue a preliminary injunction only on notice to the adverse party" (Rule 65(a)(1)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(a)(1), 65(a)(2), 65(c), 65(d)

Motion for a preliminary injunction (Rule 65(a))

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

An order restraining conduct for the duration of the lawsuit, issued after an adversarial hearing.

When to use it When you need to stop ongoing harm — e.g., enforcement of an unlawful policy — while the case is litigated.

If you miss it Harm continues pending final judgment. Delay in moving undercuts the irreparable-harm showing.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's order and by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note Rule 65(a)(2) lets the court consolidate the hearing with the trial on the merits — so evidence you present at the hearing can become part of the trial record. Rule 65(d)(1) requires every injunction to state its reasons, its terms specifically, and to describe the restrained acts "in reasonable detail—and not by referring to the complaint or other document."

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_65

Trial 4

NO deadline set by the Federal Rules. Rule 16(c)(2)(C) …
authorizes the court at a pretrial conference to consider "obtaining admissions and stipulations about facts and documents to avoid unnecessary proof, and ruling in advance on the admissibility of evidence." The actual deadline comes from the court's scheduling or final pretrial order.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 16(c)(2)(C), 16(e) (authority); Fed. R. Evid. 103 (preservation). No FRCP rule sets a filing deadline.

Motion in limine

Evidentiary / trialVerified

A pretrial motion asking the court to rule in advance that particular evidence is inadmissible, so the jury never hears it. Not created by any numbered Federal Rule of Civil Procedure.

When to use it Before trial, to exclude prejudicial material — e.g., unrelated discipline, medical history beyond the condition at issue, or settlement discussions.

If you miss it You must object at trial instead, in front of the jury, after the jury has already heard the question.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's pretrial order and by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note An in limine ruling is usually preliminary. Unless the court says the ruling is definitive, renew the objection when the evidence is actually offered to preserve the issue for appeal.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_16

"A motion for judgment as a matter of law may be made at any time before the case is submitted to the jury." The motion "must specify the judgment sought and …
the law and facts that entitle the movant to the judgment" (Rule 50(a)(2)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 50(a)(1), 50(a)(2)

Motion for judgment as a matter of law at trial (Rule 50(a))

DispositiveVerified

Asks the judge to take an issue from the jury because "a reasonable jury would not have a legally sufficient evidentiary basis to find for the party on that issue."

When to use it In a jury trial, after the opposing party has been fully heard on an issue.

If you miss it CRITICAL: failing to make a Rule 50(a) motion before the case goes to the jury forfeits the right to renew it under Rule 50(b) after the verdict, and sharply limits appellate review of evidentiary sufficiency.

Notes & source

Pro se note Make the motion on the record at the close of the opposing party's evidence AND again at the close of all evidence, and state the specific grounds. This is a preservation step, not a formality.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_50

Serve the other parties with a written demand "no later than 14 days after the last pleading directed to the issue is served," and file it in accordance with Rule 5(d) …
(Rule 38(b)). If the demand specifies only some issues, any other party may serve a demand for the remaining issues within 14 days after service of the demand (Rule 38(c)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 38(a), 38(b), 38(c), 38(d)

Jury trial demand (Rule 38(b))

ProceduralVerified

The written demand that preserves your Seventh Amendment right to a jury. Not a motion — but missing it is one of the most consequential and irreversible pro se errors.

When to use it At the earliest possible moment. Put 'JURY TRIAL DEMANDED' on the face of the complaint.

If you miss it Rule 38(d): "A party waives a jury trial unless its demand is properly served and filed." You get a bench trial. Rule 6(b) can extend it only if you move before it expires — but courts treat the Rule 38 waiver as strict.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 'last pleading directed to the issue' is usually the ANSWER, not the complaint. Demanding a jury in the caption of the complaint is the safest practice, and a withdrawal of a demand requires all parties' consent.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_38

No fixed deadline. Purely discretionary — the rule says 'may.'
Fed. R. Civ. P. 39(b); see also Fed. R. Civ. P. 39(a), 39(c)

Motion for a jury trial after failing to demand one (Rule 39(b))

ProceduralVerified

The rescue provision when a Rule 38 demand was missed: "the court may, on motion, order a jury trial on any issue for which a jury might have been demanded."

When to use it Immediately upon realizing no timely jury demand was served.

If you miss it Bench trial. Courts in many circuits are reluctant to excuse a missed demand where the only reason is inadvertence or unfamiliarity with the rules.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note File this the moment you notice the omission. Rule 39(a) also matters: even with a demand, the case is tried to the bench if the parties stipulate otherwise or the court finds there is no federal right to a jury on the issue.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_39

Post-judgment 11

"No later than 28 days after the entry of judgment—or if the motion addresses a jury issue not decided by a verdict …
no later than 28 days after the jury was discharged" (Rule 50(b)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 50(b), 50(c), 50(d); Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(2)

Renewed motion for judgment as a matter of law (Rule 50(b))

Post-judgmentVerified

Renews a Rule 50(a) motion after the verdict, asking the court to override the jury.

When to use it Only if you made a Rule 50(a) motion before the case went to the jury.

If you miss it Rule 6(b)(2) forbids ANY extension of this deadline. An untimely motion must be denied and does not toll the appeal clock under FRAP 4(a)(4).

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note Rule 50(b) expressly permits "an alternative or joint request for a new trial under Rule 59" — combine them, since both are on the same 28-day clock.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_50

"No later than 28 days after the entry of judgment, the court may amend its findings—or make additional findings—and may amend the judgment accordingly.
The motion may accompany a motion for a new trial under Rule 59" (Rule 52(b)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 52(a), 52(b); Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(2)

Motion to amend or make additional findings (Rule 52(b))

Post-judgmentVerified

After a bench trial, asks the court to amend its findings of fact and conclusions of law, or make additional ones, and amend the judgment accordingly.

When to use it After a nonjury trial where the court's findings omit or misstate a key fact.

If you miss it Rule 6(b)(2) forbids extension. The findings stand as written, and appellate review of factual findings is limited to clear error.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_52

"A motion for a new trial must be filed no later than 28 days after the entry of judgment" (Rule 59(b)).
The court may also order a new trial on its own "no later than 28 days after the entry of judgment" (Rule 59(d)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 59(a)(1), 59(a)(2), 59(b), 59(c), 59(d); Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(2)

Motion for a new trial (Rule 59(a), (b))

Post-judgmentVerified

Asks the court to set aside the verdict or decision and try the case again — for evidentiary error, an excessive or inadequate verdict, misconduct, or a verdict against the weight of the evidence.

When to use it After an adverse verdict or bench decision, where a legal error infected the trial.

If you miss it Rule 6(b)(2) forbids extension. An untimely Rule 59 motion also fails to toll the FRAP 4(a) appeal clock — losing both the motion and the appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply Rule 59(c): "When a motion for a new trial is based on affidavits, they must be filed with the motion. The opposing party has 14 days after being served to file opposing affidavits." Briefing otherwise per local rule.

Pro se note 28 days is not 30. Count it from ENTRY of judgment on the docket, and count every day including weekends and holidays under Rule 6(a)(1)(B).

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_59

"A motion to alter or amend a judgment must be filed no later than 28 days after the entry of the judgment" (Rule 59(e)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 59(e); Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(2); Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(4)(A)(iv)

Motion to alter or amend a judgment (Rule 59(e))

Post-judgmentVerified

Asks the trial court to change its own judgment — typically for a manifest error of law or fact, newly discovered evidence, or an intervening change in controlling law.

When to use it Right after an adverse summary judgment or judgment of dismissal. The main way to ask the district judge to reconsider a final ruling.

If you miss it Rule 6(b)(2) forbids extension — no judge can enlarge it. A late motion is treated as a Rule 60(b) motion, which does NOT restart the appeal clock the same way.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note A TIMELY Rule 59(e) motion resets the notice-of-appeal clock: under FRAP 4(a)(4)(A) the 30 days runs from entry of the order disposing of the motion. That makes filing within 28 days doubly important.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_59

No deadline — the court may act "whenever one is found," on motion or on its own with or without notice.
BUT after an appeal is docketed in the appellate court, such a mistake may be corrected only with the appellate court's leave.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(a)

Motion to correct a clerical mistake (Rule 60(a))

Post-judgmentVerified

Corrects "a clerical mistake or a mistake arising from oversight or omission whenever one is found in a judgment, order, or other part of the record."

When to use it For arithmetic errors, wrong names, transposed figures — not for substantive disagreement.

If you miss it Nothing — there is no time bar, but the appellate-leave requirement applies once an appeal is docketed.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_60

"A motion under Rule 60(b) must be made within a reasonable time—and for reasons (1), (2), and (3) …
no more than a year after the entry of the judgment or order or the date of the proceeding" (Rule 60(c)(1)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(b)(1)-(6), 60(c)(1), 60(c)(2), 60(d); Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(2)

Motion for relief from a final judgment or order (Rule 60(b))

Post-judgmentNo extensionVerified

A separate, later route to undo a judgment on six enumerated grounds: (1) mistake, inadvertence, surprise, or excusable neglect; (2) newly discovered evidence that with reasonable diligence could not have been discovered in time to move under Rule 59(b); (3) fraud, misrepresentation, or misconduct by an opposing party; (4) the judgment is void; (5) the judgment has been satisfied, released, or discharged, or is based on an earlier judgment that has been reversed or vacated, or applying it prospectively is no longer equitable; (6) any other reason that justifies relief.

When to use it When the 28-day Rule 59(e) window has closed, or the ground for relief (fraud, newly discovered evidence) surfaced later.

If you miss it The one-year outer limit on grounds (1)-(3) is absolute and cannot be extended (Rule 6(b)(2)). Grounds (4)-(6) remain available but only within a 'reasonable time.'

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note Rule 60(c)(2): "The motion does not affect the judgment's finality or suspend its operation" — so filing one does NOT stop the appeal clock or stay enforcement. Rule 60(d)(3) separately preserves the court's power to "set aside a judgment for fraud on the court," with no time limit.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_60

"The clerk may tax costs on 14 days' notice. On motion served within the next 7 days, the court may review the clerk's action" (Rule 54(d)(1)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 54(d)(1)

Bill of costs / motion to review the clerk's taxation of costs (Rule 54(d)(1))

Post-judgmentVerified

The prevailing party's request for taxable costs (filing fees, transcripts, copying), taxed in the first instance by the clerk.

When to use it After you prevail. Costs "other than attorney's fees—should be allowed to the prevailing party" unless a statute, rule, or court order provides otherwise.

If you miss it The clerk's taxation stands unreviewed.

Notes & source

Response / reply 7 days after the clerk's action to move for court review.

Pro se note A losing pro se plaintiff can be taxed the employer's costs. Check whether your district's local rules require a bill of costs on a particular form and within a set time.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_54

"Unless a statute or a court order provides otherwise, the motion must: (i) be filed no later than 14 days after the entry of judgment" (Rule 54(d)(2)(B)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 54(d)(2)(A), 54(d)(2)(B), 54(d)(2)(C), 54(d)(2)(D)

Motion for attorney's fees and related nontaxable expenses (Rule 54(d)(2))

Post-judgmentVerified

A separate motion claiming attorney's fees. It must specify the judgment and the statute, rule, or other grounds entitling the movant to the award, and state the amount sought or provide a fair estimate.

When to use it After prevailing under a fee-shifting statute — the ADA (42 U.S.C. § 12205) and FMLA (29 U.S.C. § 2617(a)(3)) both provide for fees.

If you miss it The fee claim is forfeited. 14 days after entry of judgment is very short and is missed constantly.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule — E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B)/(C); W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0.

Pro se note A pro se litigant who is not a lawyer generally cannot recover attorney's fees for their own time, but this deadline still matters if counsel appears at any stage — and it governs any fee motion filed against you.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_54

"Except as provided in Rule 62(c) and (d) …
execution on a judgment and proceedings to enforce it are stayed for 30 days after its entry, unless the court orders otherwise" (Rule 62(a)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 62(a)

Automatic stay of execution (Rule 62(a))

Post-judgmentVerified

An automatic pause on enforcing a money judgment immediately after it is entered, giving the losing party time to seek a stay or appeal.

When to use it Automatic — no motion needed.

If you miss it After 30 days the judgment creditor may begin execution unless a Rule 62(b) stay is in place.

Notes & source

Pro se note 30 days of automatic protection lines up with the FRAP 4(a)(1)(A) appeal deadline — but not with the 28-day Rule 59 clock. Do not assume the stay lasts through post-judgment briefing.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_62

No deadline, but it must be in place before the Rule 62(a) automatic 30-day stay expires to avoid a gap.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 62(b), 62(d), 62(e), 62(f); Fed. R. App. P. 8(a)(1)(B)

Motion for a stay by supersedeas bond or other security (Rule 62(b))

Post-judgmentVerified

Obtains a stay of enforcement pending appeal by posting a bond or other security. "The stay takes effect when the court approves the bond or other security and remains in effect for the time specified in the bond or other security."

When to use it When you are appealing a money judgment and need to stop collection.

If you miss it Collection proceeds during the appeal. Rule 62(e): no bond may be required of the United States, its officers, or its agencies. Rule 62(f) gives a judgment debtor the same stay a state court would provide.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note Rule 62(d) covers injunctions pending appeal, which is different: an injunction is NOT automatically stayed by Rule 62(c), so a separate motion is required.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_62

No deadline set by Rule 69; the procedure and any limits come from Missouri state execution law.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 69(a)(1), 69(a)(2), 69(b)

Writ of execution and discovery in aid of judgment (Rule 69)

Post-judgmentVerified

The enforcement mechanism for a money judgment. "A money judgment is enforced by a writ of execution, unless the court directs otherwise," and the procedure "must accord with the procedure of the state where the court is located."

When to use it After you win and the automatic stay has expired and the defendant will not pay.

If you miss it No collection occurs. Missouri's rules on revival of judgments will control the outer limit.

Notes & source

Pro se note Rule 69(a)(2) is the underused half: "In aid of the judgment or execution, the judgment creditor... may obtain discovery from any person—including the judgment debtor—as provided in these rules or by the procedure of the state where the court is located." You can serve interrogatories to find assets.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_69

Motion practice 2

L.R. 4.01(B): opposition memorandum "within fourteen (14) days after service of the motion";
opposition to a motion for extension of time within seven (7) days. L.R. 4.01(C): "Within ten (10) days after being served with a memorandum in opposition, the moving party may file a reply memorandum." L.R. 4.01(D): 15-page limit on any motion, memorandum, or brief (statements of material fact under (E) are exempt).
E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(A)-(E) (amended Oct. 2, 2019, effective Nov. 1, 2019)

E.D. Mo. Local Rule 4.01 — motion, opposition, reply, and summary-judgment fact statements

ProceduralVerified

The Eastern District of Missouri's motion-practice rule. It sets the response and reply clocks the FRCP leaves open, the page limit, and the summary-judgment fact-statement procedure.

When to use it Every motion filed in the Eastern District of Missouri.

If you miss it L.R. 4.01(E): "All matters set forth in the moving party's Statement of Uncontroverted Material Facts shall be deemed admitted for purposes of summary judgment unless specifically controverted by the opposing party." That single sentence decides many pro se cases.

Notes & source

Response / reply 14 days to oppose (7 days for extension motions); 10 days to reply. Additional memoranda only with leave of Court.

Pro se note In E.D. Mo. the summary-judgment fact statement is a SEPARATE filing with its own CM/ECF event ('Statement of Uncontroverted Material Facts' / 'Response to Statement of Material Facts'). Your response must set out each disputed fact with specific citations to the record and note the corresponding paragraph number from the movant's statement.

Source read www.moed.uscourts.gov/sites/moed/files/CMECF_localrule.pdf

L.R. 7.0: "Within 14 days after a motion is filed, each party opposing the motion must file suggestions opposing the motion" …
but "[f]or summary judgment motions, a party instead has 21 days." Reply suggestions: "Within 14 days after the opposing suggestions are filed." Page limits: 15 pages supporting, 15 pages opposing, 10 pages reply.
W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0; W.D. Mo. L.R. 56.1

W.D. Mo. Local Rules 7.0 and 56.1 — motion and summary-judgment practice

ProceduralVerified

The Western District of Missouri's motion-practice and summary-judgment rules, which use 'suggestions' rather than 'memoranda' and give summary-judgment oppositions extra time.

When to use it Every motion filed in the Western District of Missouri.

If you miss it L.R. 56.1 works on a 'deemed admitted' basis: facts set forth in a party's statement are deemed admitted for summary-judgment purposes unless specifically controverted by the other side.

Notes & source

Response / reply 14 days to oppose most motions; 21 days to oppose summary judgment; 14 days to reply.

Pro se note L.R. 56.1: "A party moving for summary judgment must begin its supporting suggestions with a concise statement of uncontroverted material facts" and "[e]ach fact must be set forth in a separately numbered paragraph and supported in accordance with Fed. R. Civ. P. 56(c)." Respond in the same numbered format.

Source read www.mow.uscourts.gov/sites/mow/files/Local_Rules.pdf

Filing 2

No statutory deadline; file with the complaint.
§ 1915(a)(1) requires "an affidavit that includes a statement of all assets" and "that the person is unable to pay such fees or give security therefor," plus a statement of the nature of the action and belief in entitlement to relief.
28 U.S.C. § 1915(a)(1); 28 U.S.C. § 1915(d) (officers shall issue and serve process)

Motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis (28 U.S.C. § 1915(a))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to let you file and litigate without prepaying the filing fee, based on a sworn affidavit of inability to pay listing your assets.

When to use it Filed with the complaint, at the very start of the case.

If you miss it You must pay the filing fee or the case is not docketed. Note § 1915(e)(2): the court "shall dismiss the case at any time" if the allegation of poverty is untrue, or the action is frivolous or malicious, fails to state a claim, or seeks money from an immune defendant.

Notes & source

Response / reply Decided by the court without a response; no rule-set response time.

Pro se note An IFP grant also gets the U.S. Marshals Service to serve process for you under § 1915(d) — which solves the problem that you cannot serve your own complaint.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/28/1915

No deadline. Purely discretionary — the statute says "may request," not appoint. There is no constitutional right to counsel in a civil case.
28 U.S.C. § 1915(e)(1)

Motion for appointment of counsel in a civil case (28 U.S.C. § 1915(e)(1))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to recruit a volunteer lawyer. The statute says: "The court may request an attorney to represent any person unable to afford counsel."

When to use it Any time, but strongest after the case survives dismissal and the issues prove complex — e.g., an ADA case needing expert medical proof.

If you miss it Nothing is waived; you may renew the motion as the case develops.

Notes & source

Response / reply No rule-set response time.

Pro se note Courts weigh the factual and legal complexity, your ability to investigate and present the case, and whether the claim has merit. Renewing after surviving summary judgment is often more successful than moving at filing.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/28/1915

Any stage 3

The court may extend "with or without motion or notice if the court acts, or if a request is made, before the original time or its extension expires" …
for good cause (Rule 6(b)(1)(A)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(1)(A)

Motion for extension of time — filed BEFORE the deadline (Rule 6(b)(1)(A))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to enlarge a deadline. Before the deadline runs, the standard is merely good cause.

When to use it As soon as you know you cannot meet a date — always before it passes.

If you miss it You fall to the much harder 'excusable neglect' standard of Rule 6(b)(1)(B).

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B): opposition to a motion for extension of time must be filed within SEVEN (7) days after service (not the usual 14). No separate memorandum in support is required for an extension motion; reasons must be stated in the motion body (L.R. 4.01(A)).

Pro se note E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(A): if the extension motion is by consent or unopposed, say so in the motion and it gets immediate consideration. Call opposing counsel first.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_6

The court may extend "on motion made after the time has expired if the party failed to act because of excusable neglect" (Rule 6(b)(1)(B)).
Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(1)(B)

Motion for extension of time — filed AFTER the deadline (Rule 6(b)(1)(B))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to accept a late filing. The standard rises to excusable neglect.

When to use it Only when the deadline has already passed.

If you miss it The filing is stricken as untimely; the underlying right may be lost entirely.

Notes & source

Response / reply E.D. Mo. L.R. 4.01(B): 7 days to oppose an extension motion. W.D. Mo. L.R. 7.0: 14 days.

Pro se note Explain the reason candidly and show the delay was short and caused no prejudice. 'I didn't know the rule' is weak; a documented illness or a mail failure is much stronger.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_6

"A court must not extend the time to act under Rules 50(b) and (d), 52(b), 59(b), (d), and (e), and 60(b)." In practical terms …
the 28-day deadlines for renewed JMOL, new trial, amended findings, and altering or amending a judgment — and Rule 60(b)'s time limits.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(b)(2)

Deadlines that CANNOT be extended (Rule 6(b)(2))

Time computationVerified

A short list of post-judgment deadlines the court has no power to enlarge, for any reason, no matter how good the excuse.

When to use it Check this list before relying on any extension after judgment.

If you miss it The motion is jurisdictionally untimely and must be denied. Worse, an untimely Rule 59 motion does NOT toll the appeal deadline under FRAP 4(a)(4), so you can lose the appeal too.

Notes & source

Pro se note This is the most dangerous rule in federal civil practice for a self-represented litigant. After judgment you have 28 days, full stop, and no judge can give you more.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_6

Time computation 2

For a period stated in days or a longer unit: (A) "exclude the day of the event that triggers the period";
(B) "count every day, including intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays"; and (C) "include the last day of the period, but if the last day is a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday, the period continues to run until the end of the next day that is not a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday." The last day ends, for electronic filing, at midnight in the court's time zone.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(a)(1), 6(a)(4), 6(a)(6)

Computing time — civil (Rule 6(a))

Time computationVerified

The universal counting method for every deadline in the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

When to use it Every time you calendar anything.

If you miss it Miscounting a jurisdictional deadline is not excusable.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 2009 amendments abolished the old 'skip weekends for short periods' rule. Today you count EVERY day; only the last day gets rolled forward if it lands on a weekend or holiday.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_6

Three days are added after the period would otherwise expire when service is made under Rule 5(b)(2)(C) …
(mailing), (D) (leaving with the clerk), or (F) (other means consented to). ELECTRONIC SERVICE under Rule 5(b)(2)(E) was REMOVED from Rule 6(d) by the 2016 amendment — CM/ECF service adds no extra days.
Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(d); Fed. R. Civ. P. 5(b)(2)(C), (D), (E), (F)

Three added days after service by mail (Rule 6(d))

Time computationVerified

An extra three days to act when a paper was served by a slower method.

When to use it When you were served by mail or by a consented-to method other than electronic service.

If you miss it Assuming you have 3 extra days when you were served electronically makes your filing late.

Notes & source

Pro se note If you are registered for CM/ECF, or consented to email service, you get NO extra 3 days. Only paper mail earns them. Also note the 3 days never apply to a deadline that runs from ENTRY of judgment (like Rule 59's 28 days) rather than from service.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp/rule_6

Federal criminal 17

Pretrial 8

The list: (A) a defect in instituting the prosecution …
improper venue, preindictment delay, a violation of the constitutional right to a speedy trial, selective or vindictive prosecution, an error in the grand-jury proceeding or preliminary hearing; (B) a defect in the indictment or information — joining two or more offenses in the same count (duplicity), charging the same offense in more than one count (multiplicity), lack of specificity, improper joinder, failure to state an offense; (C) suppression of evidence; (D) severance of charges or defendants under Rule 14; and (E) discovery under Rule 16. FRCrP 12(c)(1): "The court may, at the arraignment or as soon afterward as practicable, set a deadline for the parties to make pretrial motions... If the court does not set one, the deadline is the start of trial."
Fed. R. Crim. P. 12(b)(1), 12(b)(2), 12(b)(3), 12(c)(1), 12(c)(2), 12(c)(3)

Pretrial motions that MUST be made before trial (FRCrP 12(b)(3))

ProceduralVerified

A closed list of defenses, objections, and requests that are forfeited unless raised before trial, if the basis is then reasonably available and the motion can be determined without a trial on the merits.

When to use it Early, by the deadline the court sets at arraignment.

If you miss it FRCrP 12(c)(3): a motion not made by the deadline is untimely, "[b]ut a court may consider the defense, objection, or request if the party shows good cause."

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's scheduling order and local rules.

Pro se note FRCrP 12(b)(2): "A motion that the court lacks jurisdiction may be made at any time while the case is pending" — that is the one item with no deadline. FRCrP 12(c)(2) lets the court extend or reset the deadline, but only before trial.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_12

Must be made before trial (FRCrP 12(b)(3)(C)), by the deadline the court sets under FRCrP 12(c)(1); if the court sets none, the deadline is the start of trial.
Fed. R. Crim. P. 12(b)(3)(C), 12(c)(1), 12(c)(3); Fed. R. Crim. P. 41(h)

Motion to suppress evidence (FRCrP 12(b)(3)(C); FRCrP 41(h))

ProceduralVerified

Asks the court to exclude evidence obtained in violation of the Fourth, Fifth, or Sixth Amendment.

When to use it Before trial, once you know what the government seized and how.

If you miss it Untimely unless good cause is shown under FRCrP 12(c)(3); the evidence comes in.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's order and local rules.

Pro se note FRCrP 41(h): "A defendant may move to suppress evidence in the court where the trial will occur, as Rule 12 provides" — that is, in the trial court, not the district where the search happened.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_12

Must be made before trial as a Rule 12(b)(3)(D) motion, by the FRCrP 12(c)(1) deadline.
Fed. R. Crim. P. 14(a), 14(b); Fed. R. Crim. P. 12(b)(3)(D)

Motion for severance / relief from prejudicial joinder (FRCrP 14)

ProceduralVerified

Asks for separate trials of counts or of defendants when joinder is prejudicial.

When to use it In multi-count or multi-defendant cases where evidence against a codefendant would spill over.

If you miss it Untimely under FRCrP 12(c)(3) absent good cause.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's order and local rules.

Pro se note FRCrP 14(b): before ruling, "the court may order an attorney for the government to deliver to the court for in camera inspection any defendant's statement that the government intends to use as evidence" — useful where a codefendant's confession implicates you.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_14

No number of days in the rule. "The court …
by order or local rule, must set a time for the government to make its disclosures," and the time "must be sufficiently before trial to provide a fair opportunity for the defendant to meet the government's evidence." A motion to compel Rule 16 discovery must itself be made before trial under FRCrP 12(b)(3)(E).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 16(a)(1), 16(a)(2), 16(b), 16(c), 16(d)(1), 16(d)(2); Fed. R. Crim. P. 12(b)(3)(E)

Motion for discovery (FRCrP 16)

DiscoveryVerified

Requests the government's disclosure of the defendant's statements, prior record, documents and objects material to the defense, reports of examinations and tests, and expert witness disclosures. Triggers a reciprocal obligation on the defendant.

When to use it Early in the case.

If you miss it FRCrP 16(d)(2): the court may order discovery, grant a continuance, "prohibit that party from introducing the undisclosed evidence," or enter any other just order.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's order and local rules.

Pro se note Rule 16 does NOT cover witness statements (see 18 U.S.C. § 3500, the Jencks Act) or internal government work product under 16(a)(2). Brady/Giglio material comes from the Constitution, not Rule 16.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_16

No number of days. "Upon a defendant's ex parte application …
the court must order that a subpoena be issued for a named witness if the defendant shows an inability to pay the witness's fees and the necessity of the witness's presence for an adequate defense."
Fed. R. Crim. P. 17(b), 17(d), 17(e)

Subpoena for a witness at government expense (FRCrP 17(b))

ProceduralVerified

An indigent defendant's application to have witnesses subpoenaed with process costs and witness fees paid by the government.

When to use it When you cannot afford to compel a necessary witness.

If you miss it The witness cannot be compelled to appear.

Notes & source

Pro se note The application is EX PARTE — you do not have to reveal your defense strategy to the prosecution to get the subpoena.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_17

No number of days. "On motion made promptly, the court may quash or modify the subpoena if compliance would be unreasonable or oppressive" (FRCrP 17(c)(2)).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 17(c)(1), 17(c)(2), 17(c)(3), 17(g)

Subpoena for documents, and motion to quash (FRCrP 17(c))

DiscoveryVerified

A subpoena commanding a witness to produce "books, papers, documents, data, or other objects." Rule 17(c)(2) is the criminal counterpart to a motion to quash.

When to use it To obtain records from a third party for trial.

If you miss it Compliance is required; FRCrP 17(g) allows contempt for disobedience without adequate excuse.

Notes & source

Pro se note FRCrP 17(c)(3): a subpoena seeking personal or confidential information about a victim requires a court order, and the court must ordinarily give the victim notice so the victim can move to quash or modify.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_17

The detention hearing "shall be held immediately upon the person's first appearance before the judicial officer unless that person …
or the attorney for the Government, seeks a continuance." A continuance on the person's motion may not exceed five days, and on the government's motion may not exceed three days, "excluding Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays" (18 U.S.C. § 3142(f)).
18 U.S.C. § 3142(a), (c), (e), (f), (g), (i); Fed. R. Crim. P. 46(a), 46(c), 46(h), 46(j)

Detention hearing and motion for release on conditions (18 U.S.C. § 3142; FRCrP 46)

Release / detentionVerified

The hearing at which the court decides between release on recognizance or an unsecured bond, release on conditions, temporary detention, or detention under § 3142(e).

When to use it At the first appearance after arrest.

If you miss it Continued detention. FRCrP 46(c): release pending sentencing or appeal is governed by 18 U.S.C. § 3143, and "[t]he burden of establishing that the defendant will not flee or pose a danger to any other person or to the community rests with the defendant."

Notes & source

Pro se note § 3142(c) requires the LEAST restrictive condition or combination of conditions that will reasonably assure appearance and community safety. § 3142(g) lists the factors: nature and circumstances of the offense, weight of the evidence, history and characteristics of the person, and the nature and seriousness of the danger posed by release.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/3142

INDICTMENT: "Any information or indictment charging an individual with the commission of an offense shall be filed within thirty days from the date on which …
such individual was arrested or served with a summons in connection with such charges" (§ 3161(b)). TRIAL: "[T]he trial of a defendant charged in an information or indictment with the commission of an offense shall commence within seventy days from the filing date (and making public) of the information or indictment, or from the date the defendant has appeared before a judicial officer of the court in which such charge is pending, whichever date last occurs" (§ 3161(c)(1)). MINIMUM: unless the defendant consents in writing, trial "shall not commence less than thirty days from the date on which the defendant first appears through counsel or expressly waives counsel and elects to proceed pro se" (§ 3161(c)(2)).
18 U.S.C. § 3161(b), § 3161(c)(1), § 3161(c)(2), § 3161(h)

Motion to dismiss under the Speedy Trial Act (18 U.S.C. § 3161)

DispositiveVerified

Enforces the statutory clocks for indictment and trial, separate from the Sixth Amendment right.

When to use it When more than 70 non-excludable days have run before trial.

If you miss it Dismissal under 18 U.S.C. § 3162 — but the defendant must MOVE for dismissal before trial or before entering a plea, or the right is waived.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 70 days is not calendar time. § 3161(h) excludes large categories, most importantly § 3161(h)(1)(D) — delay from the filing of any pretrial motion through its disposition — and § 3161(h)(7) ends-of-justice continuances where the court finds the ends of justice outweigh the public's and defendant's interest in a speedy trial. Every motion YOU file stops the clock.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/3161

Trial 1

"After the government closes its evidence or after the close of all the evidence …
the court on the defendant's motion must enter a judgment of acquittal of any offense for which the evidence is insufficient to sustain a conviction" (FRCrP 29(a)).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 29(a), 29(b)

Motion for judgment of acquittal at trial (FRCrP 29(a))

DispositiveVerified

Asks the court to acquit because the evidence is insufficient to sustain a conviction.

When to use it After the government closes its evidence, and again after the close of all the evidence.

If you miss it Not fatal to the post-verdict motion — FRCrP 29(c)(3) says "[a] defendant is not required to move for a judgment of acquittal before the court submits the case to the jury as a prerequisite for making such a motion after jury discharge." (This differs from the civil Rule 50 sequence.)

Notes & source

Pro se note FRCrP 29(b) lets the court reserve decision and rule after the verdict — but it must then decide on the evidence as it stood when the motion was made.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_29

Post-verdict 3

"A defendant may move for a judgment of acquittal …
or renew such a motion, within 14 days after a guilty verdict or after the court discharges the jury, whichever is later" (FRCrP 29(c)(1)).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 29(c)(1), 29(c)(2), 29(c)(3), 29(d); Fed. R. Crim. P. 45(b)

Post-verdict motion for judgment of acquittal (FRCrP 29(c))

Post-convictionVerified

Renews or makes for the first time the insufficiency challenge after the verdict.

When to use it Immediately after a guilty verdict or a hung jury.

If you miss it Untimely. Unlike the civil rules, FRCrP 45(b) does allow extension for excusable neglect (its only stated exception is Rule 35), but do not rely on it.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note A timely Rule 29 motion tolls the criminal notice-of-appeal clock under FRAP 4(b)(3)(A): the 14 days to appeal runs from entry of the order disposing of it.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_29

Newly discovered evidence: "must be filed within 3 years after the verdict or finding of guilty" (FRCrP 33(b)(1)).
Any other ground: "must be filed within 14 days after the verdict or finding of guilty" (FRCrP 33(b)(2)).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 33(a), 33(b)(1), 33(b)(2)

Motion for a new trial (FRCrP 33)

Post-convictionVerified

Asks the court to vacate the judgment and grant a new trial "if the interest of justice so requires."

When to use it After conviction, on trial error or on newly discovered evidence.

If you miss it The 14-day window for non-newly-discovered grounds closes fast. Only the 3-year newly-discovered-evidence route remains.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note FRCrP 33(b)(1): "If an appeal is pending, the court may not grant a motion for a new trial until the appellate court remands the case." You may still FILE it during the appeal.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_33

"The defendant must move to arrest judgment within 14 days after the court accepts a verdict or finding of guilty …
or after a plea of guilty or nolo contendere" (FRCrP 34(b)).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 34(a), 34(b)

Motion for arrest of judgment (FRCrP 34)

Post-convictionVerified

A narrow motion: "the court must arrest judgment if the court does not have jurisdiction of the charged offense."

When to use it Rarely — only for a jurisdictional defect.

If you miss it Untimely. Note the 2014 amendment narrowed this rule — challenges to a defective indictment now belong in a pretrial FRCrP 12(b)(3)(B) motion, not a Rule 34 motion.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note A timely Rule 34 motion tolls the criminal appeal clock under FRAP 4(b)(3)(A).

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_34

Sentencing 2

"Within 14 days after sentencing, the court may correct a sentence that resulted from arithmetical, technical, or other clear error." FRCrP 35(c) …
"As used in this rule, 'sentencing' means the oral announcement of the sentence" — so the clock runs from the oral pronouncement, not entry of written judgment.
Fed. R. Crim. P. 35(a), 35(c); Fed. R. Crim. P. 45(b)(2)

Motion to correct a sentence for clear error (FRCrP 35(a))

Post-convictionNo extensionVerified

Fixes an obvious sentencing mistake without an appeal.

When to use it Immediately after sentencing, for an arithmetical, technical, or other clear error.

If you miss it FRCrP 45(b)(2): the court may not extend the time to take any action under Rule 35 except as stated in that rule. The error must then be raised on appeal or under 28 U.S.C. § 2255.

Notes & source

Pro se note The 14 days runs from the ORAL sentence. That is unusual and catches people who wait for the written judgment.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_35

"Upon the government's motion made within one year of sentencing, the court may reduce a sentence..." (FRCrP 35(b)(1)).
More than one year after sentencing, only if the assistance involved: (A) information not known to the defendant until one year or more after sentencing; (B) information provided within one year of sentencing that did not become useful to the government until more than one year after sentencing; or (C) information whose usefulness could not reasonably have been anticipated until more than a year after sentencing and which was promptly provided once its usefulness was apparent (FRCrP 35(b)(2)).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 35(b)(1), 35(b)(2), 35(c)

Motion to reduce a sentence for substantial assistance (FRCrP 35(b))

Post-convictionVerified

A GOVERNMENT motion (not the defendant's) to reduce a sentence because the defendant provided substantial assistance after sentencing.

When to use it Only the government can file it.

If you miss it No reduction. A defendant cannot compel the government to file this motion absent an unconstitutional motive or an enforceable plea-agreement promise.

Notes & source

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_35

Collateral review (§ 2255) 1

"A 1-year period of limitation shall apply to a motion under this section.
The limitation period shall run from the latest of— (1) the date on which the judgment of conviction becomes final; (2) the date on which the impediment to making a motion created by governmental action in violation of the Constitution or laws of the United States is removed, if the movant was prevented from making a motion by such governmental action; (3) the date on which the right asserted was initially recognized by the Supreme Court, if that right has been newly recognized by the Supreme Court and made retroactively applicable to cases on collateral review; or (4) the date on which the facts supporting the claim or claims presented could have been discovered through the exercise of due diligence" (§ 2255(f)).
28 U.S.C. § 2255(a), (b), (e), (f), (h)

Motion to vacate, set aside, or correct sentence (28 U.S.C. § 2255)

Post-convictionVerified

The federal prisoner's collateral attack on a sentence imposed in violation of the Constitution or federal law, by a court without jurisdiction, in excess of the maximum authorized, or otherwise subject to collateral attack. Filed in the sentencing court.

When to use it After direct appeal is over, for claims like ineffective assistance of counsel.

If you miss it Time-barred absent equitable tolling.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by the court's order under the Rules Governing Section 2255 Proceedings.

Pro se note § 2255(h): a SECOND OR SUCCESSIVE motion must first be certified by a panel of the court of appeals to contain newly discovered evidence meeting a clear-and-convincing standard, or a new retroactive rule of constitutional law. Raise every claim the first time.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/28/2255

Time computation 1

FRCrP 45(a)(1): exclude the triggering day;
"count every day, including intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays"; include the last day, rolled forward if it falls on a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday. FRCrP 45(b)(1): the court may extend before the time expires for good cause, or after it expires "if the party failed to act because of excusable neglect." FRCrP 45(b)(2): "The court may not extend the time to take any action under Rule 35, except as stated in that rule." FRCrP 45(c): three days are added after service under Civil Rule 5(b)(2)(C), (D), or (F).
Fed. R. Crim. P. 45(a), 45(b)(1), 45(b)(2), 45(c)

Computing and extending time — criminal (FRCrP 45)

Time computationNo extensionVerified

The criminal counterpart to Civil Rule 6.

When to use it Every criminal deadline.

If you miss it Untimely filings. Note the criminal rules are MORE forgiving than the civil ones — FRCrP 45(b)'s only carve-out is Rule 35, whereas Civil Rule 6(b)(2) bars extension of five separate post-judgment rules.

Notes & source

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_45

Post-judgment 1

No deadline stated in the rule. "The motion must be filed in the district where the property was seized."
Fed. R. Crim. P. 41(g)

Motion to return property (FRCrP 41(g))

ProceduralVerified

"A person aggrieved by an unlawful search and seizure of property or by the deprivation of property may move for the property's return."

When to use it To recover seized property after the criminal case ends, or where property was taken unlawfully.

If you miss it Property is retained. Note that separate civil forfeiture proceedings carry their own strict deadlines.

Notes & source

Response / reply Set by local rule.

Pro se note "The court must receive evidence on any factual issue necessary to decide the motion." If it grants the motion, the court "must return the property to the movant, but may impose reasonable conditions to protect access to the property and its use in later proceedings."

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcrmp/rule_41

Federal appellate 17

Taking the appeal 6

"[T]he notice of appeal required by Rule 3 must be filed with the district clerk within 30 days after entry of the judgment or order appealed from" (FRAP 4(a)(1)(A)).
Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(1)(A); Fed. R. App. P. 3; Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(7) (when entry occurs)

Notice of appeal — civil case (FRAP 4(a)(1)(A))

AppellateNo extensionVerified

The one-page document that starts an appeal. It is not a motion, and it is jurisdictional: file it late and the court of appeals has no power to hear you.

When to use it After a final judgment or an appealable order.

If you miss it The appeal is dismissed for lack of jurisdiction. FRAP 26(b)(1) says the court of appeals may NOT extend the time to file a notice of appeal except as authorized by Rule 4.

Notes & source

Response / reply FRAP 4(a)(3): any other party may file a notice of appeal within 14 days after the first notice is filed, or within the time otherwise allowed, whichever ends later.

Pro se note File the notice in the DISTRICT court, not the court of appeals. Count 30 days from the date the judgment is ENTERED on the docket (FRAP 4(a)(7)), not the date you received it.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4

60 days after entry of the judgment or order appealed from (FRAP 4(a)(1)(B)).
Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(1)(B)

Notice of appeal — 60 days when the United States is a party (FRAP 4(a)(1)(B))

AppellateVerified

A doubled appeal window when a federal party is involved.

When to use it When the United States, a U.S. agency, or a U.S. officer or employee sued in an official capacity (or in an individual capacity for acts connected with federal duties) is a party.

If you miss it Same jurisdictional bar as the 30-day rule.

Notes & source

Pro se note A private employer case gets 30 days, not 60. Do not assume the longer period applies just because a federal statute (FMLA/ADA) is involved — it turns on who the PARTIES are.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4

If a party timely files a motion under Civil Rule 50(b) …
52(b), 54 (for attorney's fees, if the court so orders under Rule 58), 59 (new trial or to alter/amend), or 60 (IF filed no later than 28 days after entry of judgment), then "the time to file an appeal runs for all parties from the entry of the order disposing of the last such remaining motion" (FRAP 4(a)(4)(A)).
Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(4)(A), 4(a)(4)(B)

Effect of post-judgment motions on the appeal clock (FRAP 4(a)(4))

AppellateVerified

Certain timely post-judgment motions suspend the notice-of-appeal deadline and restart it from scratch.

When to use it Whenever you file a post-judgment motion and need to know when the appeal clock actually runs.

If you miss it If the underlying motion is UNTIMELY, it does not toll anything, and the original 30-day clock keeps running. This is how litigants lose an appeal while thinking they preserved it.

Notes & source

Pro se note Note the asymmetry: a Rule 60 motion tolls the appeal clock only if filed within 28 days of judgment — the same 28 days as Rule 59(e). Outside that, a Rule 60(b) motion does not extend your time to appeal.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4

The district court may extend only if "a party so moves no later than 30 days after the time prescribed by this Rule 4(a) …
expires" and "that party shows excusable neglect or good cause." The extension may not exceed 30 days after the prescribed time or 14 days after the order granting the motion, whichever is later.
Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(5)(A), 4(a)(5)(C)

Motion to extend the time to file a notice of appeal (FRAP 4(a)(5))

AppellateNo extensionVerified

The only sanctioned way to file a late notice of appeal, decided by the DISTRICT court.

When to use it When the 30- or 60-day window has just closed and you have a genuine excuse.

If you miss it Once that additional 30 days passes, no extension is possible and the appeal right is gone.

Notes & source

Response / reply A motion filed after the original deadline expires must be served on the other parties (FRAP 4(a)(5)(B)).

Pro se note File it in the DISTRICT court. Effectively you have a hard outer limit of 60 days from judgment in an ordinary civil case — 30 to appeal plus 30 to ask for more time.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4

The district court may reopen for 14 days only if ALL of these are met …
(A) the court finds the moving party did not receive Civil Rule 77(d) notice of the entry within 21 days after entry; (B) the motion is filed "within 180 days after the judgment or order is entered or within 14 days after the moving party receives notice under Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 77(d) of the entry, whichever is earlier"; and (C) no party would be prejudiced.
Fed. R. App. P. 4(a)(6)

Motion to reopen the time to file an appeal (FRAP 4(a)(6))

AppellateVerified

A narrow rescue when you never received notice that judgment was entered.

When to use it When the clerk's notice of entry never reached you and the appeal deadline passed unknowingly.

If you miss it No reopening; the appeal is permanently lost.

Notes & source

Pro se note Keep your mailing address current with the district clerk. If you move and never get the judgment, this rule is your only remedy — and its 14-day/180-day limits are both hard.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4

"[A] defendant's notice of appeal must be filed in the district court within 14 days after the later of: (i) the entry of either the judgment or the order being appealed;
or (ii) the filing of the government's notice of appeal" (FRAP 4(b)(1)(A)). The government's notice: 30 days (FRAP 4(b)(1)(B)).
Fed. R. App. P. 4(b)(1)(A), 4(b)(1)(B), 4(b)(3)(A), 4(b)(4)

Notice of appeal — criminal case (FRAP 4(b))

AppellateVerified

The criminal defendant's notice of appeal, on a much shorter clock than civil.

When to use it After entry of the judgment of conviction or the order being appealed.

If you miss it Unlike the civil deadline, FRAP 4(b) is a claim-processing rule rather than jurisdictional in most circuits — but the government can and does enforce it, and the appeal will be dismissed if it objects.

Notes & source

Pro se note FRAP 4(b)(3)(A): a timely Criminal Rule 29, 33, or 34 motion restarts the 14 days from entry of the order disposing of the last such motion. FRAP 4(b)(4): the district court may extend up to 30 days from the expiration of the time, on a finding of excusable neglect or good cause.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_4

Appellate deadlines 1

"For good cause, the court may extend the time prescribed by these rules or by its order to perform any act, or may permit an act to be done after that time expires.
But the court may not extend the time to file: (1) a notice of appeal (except as authorized in Rule 4) or a petition for permission to appeal; or (2) a notice of appeal from or a petition to enjoin, set aside, suspend, modify, enforce, or otherwise review an order of an administrative agency, board, commission, or officer of the United States, unless specifically authorized by law."
Fed. R. App. P. 26(b)(1), 26(b)(2)

Appellate deadlines that cannot be extended (FRAP 26(b))

Time computationNo extensionVerified

The short list of appellate deadlines beyond the court's power to enlarge.

When to use it Before relying on any extension in the court of appeals.

If you miss it The court of appeals has no authority to forgive a late notice of appeal except through FRAP 4(a)(5) extension or 4(a)(6) reopening in the district court.

Notes & source

Pro se note Everything else on appeal — briefs, motions, rehearing petitions — CAN be extended for good cause. The notice of appeal cannot.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_26

Briefing 1

FRAP 31(a)(1) default: appellant "must serve and file a brief within 40 days after the record is filed";
appellee within 30 days after the appellant's brief is served; reply "within 21 days after service of the appellee's brief but a reply brief must be filed at least 7 days before argument, unless the court, for good cause, allows a later filing." These may be shortened or extended by local rule or court order. In the Eighth Circuit, 8th Cir. R. 11A states the appellant's brief is due 40 days after filing of the docket entries, and briefs are governed by the court's briefing schedule order (8th Cir. R. 31A is abrogated).
Fed. R. App. P. 31(a)(1), 31(b), 31(c); 8th Cir. R. 11A; 8th Cir. R. 28A

Briefing schedule (FRAP 31; 8th Cir. R. 11A, 28A)

AppellateVerified

The deadlines for the opening brief, response brief, and reply brief.

When to use it After the record is filed and the appeal is docketed.

If you miss it FRAP 31(c): if an appellant fails to file a brief on time, "an appellee may move to dismiss the appeal." An appellee who fails to file "will not be heard at oral argument unless the court grants permission."

Notes & source

Response / reply 40 / 30 / 21 days as above.

Pro se note Follow the Eighth Circuit's briefing schedule ORDER, not the FRAP default. 8th Cir. R. 28A(c): pro se litigants must submit one paper copy of their merits briefs by the date set in the court's briefing schedule. Motions for leave to file overlength briefs "will be granted only in extraordinary cases."

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_31

Appellate motions 3

No number of days. FRAP 8(a)(1): a party "must ordinarily move first in the district court" for a stay …
approval of a bond or other security, or an order suspending, modifying, restoring, or granting an injunction. FRAP 8(a)(2): a motion in the court of appeals must show "that moving first in the district court would be impracticable" or that the district court denied the motion or failed to afford the relief requested, and must state the reasons the district court gave.
Fed. R. App. P. 8(a)(1), 8(a)(2), 8(b), 8(c); Fed. R. Civ. P. 62

Motion for stay or injunction pending appeal (FRAP 8)

Emergency / injunctiveVerified

Asks to suspend a judgment or obtain an injunction while the appeal is pending.

When to use it When enforcement during the appeal would cause irreparable harm.

If you miss it The judgment or injunction is enforced during the appeal.

Notes & source

Response / reply Motion practice per FRAP 27: response within 10 days after service; reply within 7 days after service of the response.

Pro se note Going straight to the Eighth Circuit without first asking the district judge is the most common reason these are denied. Ask below first, and attach the district court's ruling.

Source read www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap/rule_8

which is filed in the DISTRICT court with an affidavit showing inability to pay, claiming entitlement to redress, and stating the issues intended to be presented. If the district court denies it, a motion in the court of appeals must be filed "within 30 days after service of the notice" of the district court's denial (FRAP 24(a)(5)).

R. 40A: "Successive petitions for rehearing are not allowed."

If you miss it In a civil case the 90-day period comes from § 2101(c) and the Court treats it as jurisdictional — a late petition is dismissed and the most a Justice can add is the 60 days in Rule 13.5. Count from entry of judgment, not from the mandate.

Notes & source
